The multiplicative factor
\eta^{\uparrow}_{2}
is brought by the overlap of more than two fermion lines of the same spin state. Consider the noninteracting case. While
(A)
contributes, the diagrams
(B)
and
(C)
vanish collectively once the sum over paths (specifically over
\eta^{\uparrow}_{2}
) is performed, as the phase factor
\mathcal{B}
in the propagator is independent of the sojourn indexes, see Eq. (
42
) and Fig.
7
. In the interacting case,
(C)
contributes because there is a phase factor associated to
\eta^{\uparrow}_{2}
due to the sojourn-blip overlap, while
(B)
is still vanishing, as the sojourn-sojourn overlap does not contribute to the phase factors, see also Ref.
O. Karlström et al. 2013
.

V
Generalized master equation for the populations and the current

In the absence of time-dependent driving, the propagator has time-translation symmetry. It is therefore convenient to Laplace-transform the population propagator order by order and obtain a generalized master equation (GME) for the populations and an integral equation for the current. The kernels of these equations are related to each other and, in turn, connected to the Green’s functions. This connection will be elaborated in Sec.
VII
.
In the following, we indicate as
\hat{f}(\lambda)=\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\exp(-\lambda t)f(t)
the Laplace transform of a function
f(t)
.

V.1
GME for the populations

Due to the nested time integrals in the definition of
J^{(m)}
, Eq. (
40
), the reducible contributions, i.e. the ones that can be cut by a vertical line not crossing any fermion line, factorize in Laplace space. For this reason, the populations and current kernels collect the so-called irreducible diagrammatic contributions, the ones that cannot be cut by a vertical line not crossing any fermion line.

Using Eq. (
48
), the zeroth-order contribution to the propagator is

J^{(0)}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(t;0)=\delta_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\;,

(61)

and its Laplace transform reads

\hat{J}^{(0)}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\lambda}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\;.

(62)

Let us denote with
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}
the set of sojourn indexes associated to all states except
i
, namely

\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}=(\dots,\eta^{i-1},\eta^{i+1},\dots)

and set the initial time
t_{0}=0
.
The term
m=1
contains two tunneling transitions. This implies the change in the occupation of one state at most. Thus, the first-order propagator has composite indexes
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}
that differ for at most one entry.
The resulting first-order propagator can be readily calculated according to the definition, Eq. (
40
), and the diagrammatic rules set up in Sec.
IV.4
, yielding

\displaystyle J^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(t;0)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\int_{0}^{t}dt_{2}\int_{0}^{t_{2}}dt_{1}\sum_{\zeta^{i}}e^{-\frac{\rm i}{\hbar}\zeta^{i}E_{i}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)})(t_{2}-t_{1})}

(63)

\displaystyle\times{\eta^{\prime}}^{i}\eta^{i}\;[-{\rm g}_{-\eta^{i}}^{-\zeta^{i}}(t_{2}-t_{1})]\delta_{{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i)}}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}\;.

If, for some
i
,
{\eta^{\prime}}^{i}\neq\eta^{i}
, we have
{\eta^{\prime}}^{i}\eta^{i}=-1
because
\eta=\pm 1
. Moreover, the sum over
i
collapses to a single term due to the constraint
\delta_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}
.
Taking into account Eq. (
61
), this implies that, up to first order,

J_{\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(t;0)=1-\sum_{i}J^{({\eta^{\prime}}^{i}\neq\eta^{i})}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(t;0)\;,

in agreement with the conservation of the total probability.

Let us introduce the irreducible kernel of order
1
using the explicit form for the correlator, Eq. (
29
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\tau)=

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{N}{\eta^{\prime}}^{j}\eta^{j}\sum_{i\zeta^{i}\alpha k\sigma}e^{-\frac{\rm i}{\hbar}\zeta^{i}[E_{i}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)})-\epsilon_{k}]\tau}

(64)

\displaystyle\times\frac{-|{\rm t}_{i\alpha k\sigma}|^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}f^{\alpha}_{-\eta^{i}}(\epsilon_{k})\delta_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i)}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}\;,

where we singled out the prefactor
\prod_{j}{\eta^{\prime}}^{j}\eta^{j}=\pm 1
, common to all orders (see the diagrammatic rules), by exploiting the property that when two sojourn indexes are the same they contribute as
(\eta^{i})^{2}=1
.
In Laplace space, the first-order propagator acquires then the form

\displaystyle\hat{J}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\lambda)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\lambda)\frac{1}{\lambda}\;,

(65)

with

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\lambda)=

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{N}{\eta^{\prime}}^{j}\eta^{j}\sum_{i\zeta^{i}\alpha k\sigma}\frac{-(|{\rm t}_{i\alpha k\sigma}|^{2}/\hbar^{2})\;f^{\alpha}_{-\eta^{i}}(\epsilon_{k})}{\lambda+{\rm i}\zeta^{i}[E_{i}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)})-\epsilon_{k}]/\hbar}\delta_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i)}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}\;.

(66)

If, for some
i
,
{\eta^{\prime}}^{i}\neq\eta^{i}
, then the prefactor is
-1
and the sum over
i
collapses to a single term, as for the first-order propagator. This entails that,
from Eqs. (
63
) and (
65
), the diagonal elements of the irreducible kernel are related to the off-diagonal ones by

\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\lambda)=-\sum_{i}\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{(1),{\eta^{\prime}}^{i}\neq\eta^{i}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\lambda)\;.

(67)

Since, as we show below, the rates
\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0)
are the steady-state rates of the master equation for the populations in the sequential tunneling approximation
Koller et al. 2010
, Eq. (
67
) is consistent with the conservation of the total probability.

Higher-order contributions can be calculated as well according to the diagrammatic rules given in Sec.
IV.4
. The
2
nd-order contribution to the propagator for the populations is the sum of the three classes of diagrams in Fig.
8
.

Figure 8:
The three topologies of
2
nd-order diagrams. Each fermion line can belong to each of the
N
states of the central system S. The first diagram is reducible while the second and third are irreducible.

The first is a reducible diagram and in Laplace space is the product of two lower-order diagrams

\displaystyle\hat{J}_{\includegraphics[height,angle]{diagram_2nd-red.pdf},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{(2)}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\lambda}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime\prime}}\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime\prime}}(\lambda)\frac{1}{\lambda}\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\lambda)\frac{1}{\lambda}\;.

(68)

Here, the internal sum over
\boldsymbol{\eta}
has been singled-out, allowing for the use of the matrix notation

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}_{\includegraphics[height,angle]{diagram_2nd-red.pdf}}^{(2)}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\lambda}\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}\cdot\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}\;.

(69)

The full propagating function
\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}^{(2)}
in Laplace space, expressed as the sum of the three diagrammatic contributions shown in Fig.
8
, is given by

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}^{(2)}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\lambda}\left[\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}\cdot\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}+\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(2)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}\right]\;,

(70)

where the irreducible kernel of
2
nd order
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(2)}(\lambda)
is the sum of the two
2
nd-order irreducible diagrams in Fig.
8
(the second and the third) in Laplace space. As discussed in Sec.
VI
below, these contributions can be written as the contraction of a matrix block with a vertex, as in Eq. (
66
), with the difference that the block has now internal processes. The same applies to higher-order irreducible kernels leading to the final Eq. (
97
) below.

\hbox to171.52pt{\vbox to33.32pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.62637pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

. The ten diagrams on the right are irreducible and their explicit forms are the same as the ones listed in Appendix

}\end{gathered}

(72)

The crosses have the role of exchanging the fermion lines to produce the different topologies of diagrams.
In the first column of Fig.
9
are listed the reducible
3
rd-order diagrams that can be obtained by combining the two
2
nd-order irreducible diagrams in Fig.
8
and a
1
st-order one. The second column of Fig.
9
lists the irreducible diagrams divided for convenience in the two classes with overlap of two and three fermion lines. Irreducible diagrams containing
n
overlapping fermion lines are called
n
-tier diagrams.

Along the same lines as with the
2
nd order, we can write the
3
rd order propagating function in Laplace space as the sum of products of lower-order irreducible kernels plus the irreducible
3
rd order kernel
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(3)}(\lambda)
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}^{(3)}(\lambda)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\Bigg[\left(\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}\right)^{3}+\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}\cdot\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(2)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}

(73)

\displaystyle+\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(2)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}\cdot\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}+\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(3)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}\Bigg]\;,

where
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(3)}(\lambda)
collects the irreducible
3
rd order diagrams in Fig.
9
(the ones in the second column).

At this point we are in the position to derive the formally exact GME for the populations and the current.
The exact propagator is obtained by summing over all orders
m
the
m
th-order propagators as follows

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}

\displaystyle(\lambda)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}^{(m)}(\lambda)

(74)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\Bigg[\mathbf{1}+\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}+\left(\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}\right)^{2}+\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(2)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}\right)^{3}+2\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}\cdot\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(2)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}+\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(3)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}+\dots\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}+\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(2)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}+\dots\right)^{m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}(\lambda)}{\lambda}\right)^{m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\lambda\mathbf{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}(\lambda)\right]^{-1}\;,

where we introduced the kernel

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}(\lambda)=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(m)}(\lambda)\;

(75)

which collects all the irreducible contributions to
\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}
.
From Eq. (
74
),
\lambda\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}(\lambda)-\mathbf{1}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}(\lambda)\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}(\lambda)
.
By transforming back to the time domain, this implies that
\boldsymbol{J}
is the solution of the following GME

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\boldsymbol{J}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\;\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}(t-t^{\prime})\cdot\boldsymbol{J}(t^{\prime})\;.

(76)

According to Eq. (
19
), the populations are obtained by multiplying the above matrix equation by
\mathbf{P}(0)
, the population vector at the initial time
t=0
, which results in

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{P}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\;\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}(t-t^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{P}(t^{\prime})\;.

(77)

The asymptotic populations are the solution of the equation
\mathbf{0}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}(0)\cdot\mathbf{P}^{\infty}
, which is obtained upon applying to Eq. (
77
) the final value theorem
f(t\rightarrow\infty)=\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\lambda\hat{f}(\lambda)
.

V.2
Integral equation for the current

In the present situation of diagonal hybridization matrices, the current functional
\mathcal{I}_{l}
, Eq. (
13
), entering the expression for the current through the lead
l
via Eq. (
12
), specializes to

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{l}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{*},\boldsymbol{\xi},\bar{\boldsymbol{\xi}})=

\displaystyle-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}dt^{\prime}\sum_{i,x,y,z}\left[x\;\xi_{y}^{z}(t){\rm g}_{l,xz}^{-z}(t-t^{\prime})\xi_{x}^{-z}(t^{\prime})\right]_{i}

(78)

\displaystyle\times\delta_{z,-1}\delta_{y,+1}\;.

The current functional has thus the same form as the phase of the influence functional except that there is no integration over the time of the last tunneling transition and there are constrains on the contributing processes. This entails that the diagrammatic unraveling of the current propagator, obtained by expanding the influence functional as a series in the tunneling transitions, goes along the same lines as the one for the populations. The differences consist in the
last
fermion line of the diagrams bearing the constraints associated with the current and the nested time integrals missing the integration over the last tunneling time.
The expansion of
J^{I}_{l,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(t,t_{0})
starts from
m=1
because, not being at the exponent, the current functional adds two additional transitions to the ones generated by expanding the influence functional. This also implies that, if there are no coherences in the initial state of the system, then also the paths contributing to the current start and end in sojourn states.
The current propagator, in the discretized picture and in the occupation number representation, is then

J^{I}_{l,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(t;0)=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}J^{I(m)}_{l,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(t;0)
, where

\displaystyle J^{I(m)}_{l,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(t;0)=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\rm paths}_{m}}\int\mathcal{D}^{I}\{t\}_{m}\prod_{i}\sum_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}\mathcal{B}_{m_{i}}^{i}(\mathcal{P}_{i}){\Phi}_{m_{i}}^{i}(\mathcal{P}_{i})

(79)

\displaystyle\times{\rm constraints}\;,

with
\sum_{i}m_{i}=m
and
\mathcal{B}_{m_{i}}^{i}
and
\Phi_{m_{i}}^{i}
defined in Eq. (
41
).
To order
m
there are
2m
tunneling transitions, as for the populations, however in the case of the current the last transition occurs at the final time
t
, yielding the definition

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{D}^{I}\{t\}_{m}:=

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t}dt_{2m-1}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{2m-1}}dt_{2m-2}\dots\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{2}}dt_{1}\;.

(80)

Going to the details of the constrains in the current calculations, they can be read-off from Eq. (
78
). The first is that the correlator of the last fermion line is not summed over the leads but has the index
l
of the considered lead. Further,
according to Eq. (
32
) the Grassmann variable associated to the last transition (the paths start from and land in a sojourn) is of the type
\xi^{\zeta^{\prime}}_{-\eta^{\prime}\zeta^{\prime}}
. Then, the constraints in Eq. (
78
) translate into

\zeta^{\prime}=-1
and
-\eta^{\prime}\zeta^{\prime}=+1
, which imply
\delta_{\eta^{\prime},+1}
, namely the last sojourn of the degree of freedom
i
associated to the last transition has to be
+1
.
Summarizing, the current constraints on the last fermion line are

•

The last fermion line is specific to the lead
l
so that there is no contraction over the lead index
\alpha^{\prime}
.

•

The index
\zeta^{\prime}
of the last fermion line is constrained to be
\zeta^{\prime}=-1
.

•

The final sojourn of the state
i
associated to the fermion line making the last transition must be
{\eta^{\prime}}^{i}=+1
(the sum over
i
accounts for all possible processes).

Examples of paths that contribute to
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{l}
with associated fermion lines are shown in Fig.
10
.

i,l
{\eta^{\prime}}^{i}=+1

i,l
{\eta^{\prime}}^{i}=+1

i,l
{\eta^{\prime}}^{i}=+1

i,l
{\eta^{\prime}}^{i}=+1

Figure 10:
Examples of diagrams contributing to
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{l}
. The sojourn of the degree of freedom
i
associated to the last transition is constrained to be
{\eta^{i}}^{\prime}=+1
.

The explicit expression for the term
m=1
in the expansion of the current propagator is

\displaystyle J^{I(1)}_{l,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(t;0)=

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{N}{\eta^{\prime}}^{j}\eta^{j}\sum_{i}\int_{0}^{t}dt_{1}\sum_{\zeta^{i}}e^{-\frac{\rm i}{\hbar}\zeta^{i}E_{i}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)})(t_{2}-t_{1})}

(81)

\displaystyle\times\;[-{\rm g}_{l,-\eta^{i}}^{-\zeta^{i}}(t_{2}-t_{1})]\delta_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i)}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}\delta_{\zeta^{i},-1}\delta_{{\eta^{\prime}}^{i},+1}

(cf. Eq. (
63
)). In Laplace space

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}^{I(1)}_{l}(\lambda)=

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{I(1)}_{l}(\lambda)\frac{1}{\lambda}\;.

(82)

Let us denote with
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}_{l}^{I}(\lambda)=\sum_{m}\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}_{l}^{I(m)}(\lambda)
the sum of all the irreducible diagrammatic contributions to
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}_{l}(\lambda)
with the last fermion line satisfying the constraints given by the current functional. Note that the reducible contributions to
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}_{l}(\lambda)
are products of ordinary irreducible kernels
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}(\lambda)
with only the last factor of the type
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}_{l}^{I}(\lambda)
. This is because only the last fermion line bears the constraints of the current calculation. Then we find that the exact current propagator is the following sum over all orders
m
of the
m
-th order propagators

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}}_{l}(\lambda)=

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}_{l}^{I}(\lambda)}{\lambda}\cdot\Bigg[\mathbf{1}+\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}+\left(\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}\right)^{2}+\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(2)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}

(83)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}\right)^{3}+\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}\cdot\frac{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(2)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}+\dots\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}_{l}^{I}(\lambda)\cdot\left[\lambda\mathbf{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}(\lambda)\right]^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}_{l}^{I}(\lambda)\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}(\lambda)\;,

or, in the time domain

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{l}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\;\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}_{l}^{I}(t-t^{\prime})\cdot\boldsymbol{J}(t^{\prime})\;.

(84)

Similarly to the steady state-populations, the steady-state current is found by applying to Eq. (
83
) the final value theorem, which results in

\displaystyle I_{l}^{\infty}=

\displaystyle e2{\rm Re}\;{\rm Tr}_{\rm S}[\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}_{l}^{\infty}]

(85)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\;e2{\rm Re}\;{\rm Tr}_{\rm S}[\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}_{l}^{I}(\lambda)\cdot\lambda\hat{\boldsymbol{J}}(\lambda)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e2{\rm Re}\;{\rm Tr}_{\rm S}[\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}_{l}^{I}(0)\cdot\boldsymbol{J}^{\infty}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e2{\rm Re}\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\hat{\mathcal{K}}_{l,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{I}(0){\rm P}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\infty}\;,

where we assumed that the matrix elements of the asymptotic propagator
\boldsymbol{J}^{\infty}
are independent of their column index, i.e. that the steady-state populations are independent of their initial values. In other words, in the asymptotic propagator matrix each column is equal to the asymptotic population vector.

VI
Exact formal expression for the kernel

VI.1
Block structure of the irreducible kernel

The diagrammatic contributions to the populations and current propagators display an exponential dependence on time, cf. Eqs. (
29
) and (
42
). This feature and the nested time integrals enable one to express the irreducible kernels in Laplace space as the
contraction
of products of blocks - each equipped with a matrix structure - with an initial vertex.
The simplest example is provided by the first-order irreducible kernel
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{(1)}(\lambda)
in Eq. (
66
), which can be rendered by the contraction of the product of two matrices, associated to an initial vertex
\mathbf{v}
and a block
\mathbf{h}(\lambda)
which encompasses a free fermion line, respectively, see Fig.
11
.

\mathbf{h}
t_{1}
t_{2}
\mathbf{v}\hskip 11.74988pt

Figure 11:
Irreducible diagram of order
1
. The vertex
\mathbf{v}
is denoted by the full dot. The two ends of the fermion line are contracted, namely the indexes
\zeta^{i},\alpha
,
k
, and
\sigma
are summed over. The time interval
t_{2}-t_{1}
is a blip only for the state associated to the fermion line. Note that the states
i
are summed over in contracting the fermion line, see Eq. (
90
).

The matrix blocks are indexed by the state index
i
and the associated collective index

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\chi}:=(\underbrace{\zeta^{i},\alpha,k,\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)})

(86)

which includes path and leads variables (note that the components of
\chi
depend on the state
i
).
The scalar product between two generic blocks
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
is given by

[\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{B}]^{i^{\prime}i}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\chi}}=\sum_{i^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\prime\prime}}[\mathbf{A}]^{i^{\prime}i^{\prime\prime}}_{\boldsymbol{\chi^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\chi^{\prime\prime}}}[\mathbf{B}]^{i^{\prime\prime}i}_{\boldsymbol{\chi^{\prime\prime}}\boldsymbol{\chi}}\;.

(87)

We denote by the symbol
\langle\cdot\rangle
the contraction of a matrix block
\mathbf{C}
with an initial vertex
{\rm v}_{-\eta^{i}}
. The contraction consists in summing over the initial and final index
\boldsymbol{\kappa}
and
\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}
, cf. Eq. (
86
), namely

\langle\mathbf{C}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{-\eta^{i}}\rangle^{i^{\prime}i}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}[\mathbf{C}]^{i^{\prime}i}_{\boldsymbol{\chi^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\chi}}{\rm v}^{i}_{-\eta^{i}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})\;.

(88)

As a result, the first-order kernel in Eq. (
66
) can be written as

\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\lambda)=\prod_{j=1}^{N}{\eta^{\prime}}^{j}\eta^{j}\sum_{i^{\prime}i}\langle\mathbf{h}(\lambda)\cdot\mathbf{v}_{-\eta^{i}}\rangle^{i^{\prime}i}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}\;,

(89)

which is the contraction of the scalar product

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{h}(\lambda)\cdot\mathbf{v}_{-\eta^{i}}\rangle^{i^{\prime}i}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\kappa}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\sum_{i^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\prime\prime}}[\mathbf{h}(\lambda)]^{i^{\prime}i^{\prime\prime}}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\chi^{\prime\prime}}}[\mathbf{v}_{-\eta^{i}}]^{i^{\prime\prime}i}_{\boldsymbol{\chi^{\prime\prime}}\boldsymbol{\chi}}\;.

(90)

Here, we have defined the matrix blocks of elements

\displaystyle[\mathbf{h}(\lambda)]^{i^{\prime}i}_{\boldsymbol{\chi^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\chi}}:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda+{\rm i}\zeta^{i}[E_{i}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)})-\epsilon_{k}]/\hbar}\delta_{i^{\prime}i}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\chi^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\chi}}\;,

(91)

\displaystyle[\mathbf{v}_{\pm\eta^{i}}]^{i^{\prime}i}_{\boldsymbol{\chi^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\chi}}:=

\displaystyle-\frac{|{\rm t}_{i\alpha\sigma}(\epsilon_{k})|^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}f^{\alpha}_{\pm\eta^{i}}(\epsilon_{k})\delta_{i^{\prime}i}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\chi^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\chi}}

\displaystyle\equiv{\rm v}^{i}_{\pm\eta^{i}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})\delta_{i^{\prime}i}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\chi^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\chi}}\;.

Graphically, the vertexes
{\rm v}_{\pm}
are associated to the two processes

}\end{gathered}\;{\rm v}_{+\eta^{i}}\;,

(92)

with the
\pm
sign of
{\rm v}
being established directly by the form of the influence functional. Note that, since we deal with the population propagator, the paths start and end in sojourns, thus the initial vertex is always of the type
{\rm v}_{-\eta}
.
In all diagrams, the two ends of each fermion line are contracted in this way.
Analogously, the matrix element of the irreducible current kernel of first order
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}^{I(1)}_{l}(\lambda)
reads

[\hat{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}}}^{I(1)}_{l}(\lambda)]_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}=\prod_{j=1}^{N}{\eta^{\prime}}^{j}\eta^{j}\sum_{i^{\prime}i}\delta_{{\eta^{\prime}}^{i},+1}\langle{\rm c}^{\prime}_{l}\mathbf{h}(\lambda)\cdot\mathbf{v}_{-\eta^{i}}\rangle^{i^{\prime}i}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}\;,

(93)

where
{\rm c}^{\prime}_{l}:=\delta_{{\zeta^{\prime}}^{i},-1}\delta_{\alpha^{\prime},l}
, so that

[{\rm c}^{\prime}_{l}\mathbf{h}(\lambda)]^{i^{\prime}i}_{\boldsymbol{\chi^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\chi}}:=\frac{\delta_{\zeta^{i},-1}\delta_{\alpha,l}}{\lambda+{\rm i}\zeta^{i}[E_{i}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)})-\epsilon_{k}]/\hbar}\delta_{i^{\prime}i}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\chi^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\chi}}\;,

(94)

cf. Eq. (
91
).

. A vertex is denoted by a full dot. The two ends of the lines connecting two blocks carry path indexes that are summed over in the connection. The first block is contracted with the vertex
\mathbf{v}
and the right end of the last block is also contracted.

The free propagator, bubble, and crossing blocks involved in the diagrams of Figs.

.

Going to orders higher than the first, consider the four-transition diagrams of Fig.
12
: The time slicing yields a block-product structure in Laplace space.
The blocks are shown in Fig.
13
. Each of them is a matrix with state indexes
i,j
and the two collective indexes
\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{\chi}
that specify the path and lead variables according to Eq. (
86
).

The sum over paths is performed automatically by the matrix multiplications implied by forming the diagrams from blocks which, in turn, possibly contain internal processes. The simplest examples of the latter are given by the bubble and crossing blocks,
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{X}
, of Fig.
13
.
The irreducible kernel of order
2

in Laplace space acquires the following expressions in terms of the blocks defined above

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{(2)}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}=\prod_{j=1}^{N}{\eta^{\prime}}^{j}\eta^{j}\sum_{i^{\prime}i}\langle

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}\cdot\Big[\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{X}\Big]\cdot\mathbf{h}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{-\eta^{i}}\rangle^{i^{\prime}i}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}\;,

(95)

where the dependences on
\lambda
are understood.

The bubble and the crossing shown in Fig.
13
constitute the building blocks of the important resonant tunneling approximation (RTA)
J. König et al. 1996
in which diagrams with overlap of more than two overlapping fermion lines are neglected, see Eq. (
106
) below.
The
3
rd order irreducible kernel in Laplace space reads, within the RTA,

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{(3)}_{{\rm RTA}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}=

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{N}{\eta^{\prime}}^{j}\eta^{j}\sum_{i^{\prime}i}\langle\mathbf{h}\cdot\Big[\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{X}\Big]\cdot\mathbf{h}

(96)

\displaystyle\cdot\Big[\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{X}\Big]\cdot\mathbf{h}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{-\eta^{i}}\rangle^{i^{\prime}i}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}\;.

Including in a formal way the contributions beyond-RTA and the higher-order irreducible diagrams with overlap of arbitrarily many fermion lines, we obtain a picture where the kernel results from contraction of a dressed block, the irreducible propagator
\boldsymbol{\phi}
, whose definition is the object of the next section.

VI.2
Diagrammatic unraveling of the kernel

The sum of all irreducible diagrams can be obtained by contracting with an initial vertex the dressed block
\boldsymbol{\phi}
which gives the exact kernel according to

\hat{\mathcal{K}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\lambda)=\prod_{j=1}^{N}{\eta^{\prime}}^{j}\eta^{j}\sum_{i^{\prime}i}\langle\boldsymbol{\phi}(\lambda)\cdot\mathbf{v}_{-\eta^{i}}\rangle^{i^{\prime}i}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}\;.

(97)

The irreducible propagator
\boldsymbol{\phi}
can be seen as a block with an incoming and an outgoing fermion line, similar to the ones shown in Fig.
13
, dressed by processes of all orders. Using the notation introduced in Sec.
V
, with the boxes that permute the fermion lines numbered according to the number of overlapping lines, we can give the following (symbolic) exact expression

(98)

Notice that, for simplicity,
the diagrams and the corresponding formulas above them are both ordered from left to right here, at variance with the convention used throughout the text, where formulas are ordered from right to left.
This formal unraveling of the propagator constitutes a main result of the present work. It systematically encompasses the truncation schemes based on the order in
\Gamma
(e.g. sequential tunneling and cotunneling) and on the depth of the hierarchy of overlap of fermion lines (e.g. RTA), as we exemplify below. In Eq. (
98
) we have introduced
\mathbf{h}_{1}\equiv\mathbf{h}
and similarly, for the blocks with higher overlap of noncrossing fermion lines,
\mathbf{h}_{n}
.
As in the definition of the first-order irreducible kernel in Eq. (
90
), the symbol
\langle\cdot\rangle
implies the contraction with an initial vertex of a fermion line, in this case the most internal. The boxes
\boxed{2}
and
\boxed{3}
are defined in Eqs. (
71
) and (
72
), respectively. The box
\boxed{4}
reads

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 23.62 L 31.5 23.62}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 39.37 L 31.5 39.37}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 55.12 L 31.5 7.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}\end{gathered}\;,

(99)

and analogous definitions hold for the higher-order boxes. Finally,
\mathbf{S}_{n}
contains the box
\boxed{n}
that operates on
n
fermion lines by exchanging them in pairs and thus represent a class of blocks.

From the schematic expression in Eq. (
98
) we find in Laplace space

\boldsymbol{\phi}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{h}\langle\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{2}\mathbf{v}\rangle\right)^{n}\mathbf{h}

(100)

with
\langle\mathbf{S}_{2}\mathbf{v}\rangle\equiv\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{X}
, the RTA self-energy, and
\mathbf{h}_{1}\equiv\mathbf{h}
, see Fig.
13
.
In Eq. (
100
), we have defined the dressed self-energies
iteratively
as

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{n-1}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Bigg(\mathbf{h}_{n-1}\langle\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{n}\mathbf{v}\rangle\Bigg)^{k}\mathbf{S}_{n-1}\;.

(101)

Summing the geometrical series in Eq. (
100
) we find the exact result

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\phi}=[\mathbf{h}^{-1}-\langle\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{2}\mathbf{v}\rangle]^{-1}\;.

(102)

Thus, the function
\boldsymbol{\phi}
is the solution of the Dyson equation

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\phi}=\mathbf{h}+\mathbf{h}\langle\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{2}\mathbf{v}\rangle\boldsymbol{\phi}\;.

(103)

The formal exact expression for
\boldsymbol{\phi}
generates the different approximations schemes used in literature by suitably truncating the series expression, Eq. (
100
), and/or the depth of the hierarchy in Eq. (
101
). For example, the sequential tunneling (ST) scheme, first order in
\Gamma
, is recovered by truncating Eq. (
100
) to
n=0
,

\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm ST}=\mathbf{h}\;.

(104)

The next-order perturbative scheme includes the cotunneling (CT) diagrams, with
n=0,1
in Eq. (
100
) and
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{2}=\mathbf{S}_{2}
, so that
\langle\mathbf{S}_{2}\mathbf{v}\rangle=\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{X}
. Then the irreducible propagator reads

\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm CT}=\mathbf{h}+\mathbf{h}(\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{X})\mathbf{h}\;.

(105)

Retaining all orders in the series expansion for
\boldsymbol{\phi}
and truncating the hierarchy in Eq. (
101
) to two overlapping fermion lines, i.e. to the second tier, where
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{2}=\mathbf{S}_{2}
, we obtain the Dyson equation for the RTA propagator

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm RTA}=\mathbf{h}+\mathbf{h}(\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{X})\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm RTA}\;.

(106)

This approximation scheme is nonperturbative in the tunnel coupling
\Gamma
and corresponds to the scheme proposed in
J. König et al. 1996
;
O. Karlström et al. 2013
. Note that the propagator
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm CT}
is readily recovered by expanding
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm RTA}
to order
\Gamma^{2}
. From the RTA, neglecting the crossing block, we obtain the second-tier noncrossing approximation (NCA2), to be discussed below, which is the solution of

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm NCA2}=\mathbf{h}+\mathbf{h}\mathbf{B}\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm NCA2}

(107)

and reproduces the results obtained by Meir, Wingreen, and Lee in
Meir et al. 1991
with the equation of motion method.

VI.3
Dyson equation for the kernel in terms of dressed bubbles and crossings

We split the exact, dressed self-energy of Eqs. (
102
) and (
103
) as
\langle\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{2}\mathbf{v}\rangle=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}+\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
, according to whether the incoming and outgoing fermion lines are the same or not.
This yields

\boldsymbol{\phi}=\left[\mathbf{h}^{-1}-(\tilde{\mathbf{B}}+\tilde{\mathbf{X}})\right]^{-1}\;.

(108)

Hence, the sum
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}+\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
is the self-energy for the exact propagator
\boldsymbol{\phi}
.
The irreducible block
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
is a fermion line dressed by processes of all orders, including crossings. In the dressed, irreducible crossing block
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
, the incoming and outgoing fermion lines are not the same, which implies the presence of crossings involving these main fermion lines. These two dressed blocks are shown in Fig.
14
.

\;\;i\;\boldsymbol{\kappa}
i\;\boldsymbol{\kappa}
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}
{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i)}
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}

\;\;i\;\boldsymbol{\kappa}
i^{\prime}\;\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}
{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i^{\prime})}
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}

Figure 14:
Dressed, irreducible bubble and crossing blocks that generalize the ones shown in Fig.
13
. The sum
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}+\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
is the self-energy for the exact propagator
\boldsymbol{\phi}
. Within the dressed bubble approximation (DBA), the irreducible bubble diagram
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
is the self-energy of the propagator
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm DBA}
.

Let us introduce the dressed propagator in the dressed bubble approximation (DBA),
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm DBA}
, obtained by setting to zero the crossing block
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
. It satisfies the same Dyson equation as
\boldsymbol{\phi}
, but with the dressed bubble self-energy alone; namely, in Laplace space,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm DBA}=

\displaystyle\left[\mathbf{h}^{-1}-\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\right]^{-1}\;,

(109)

or equivalently

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm DBA}=

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}+\mathbf{h}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm DBA}\;.

(110)

Truncation of the hierarchy to the second tier yields the NCA2, Eq. (
107
), where
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\equiv\mathbf{B}
.
In terms of
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm DBA}
, we can cast the exact equation for
\boldsymbol{\phi}
, Eq. (
103
), in the form

\boldsymbol{\phi}=\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm DBA}+\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm DBA}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}\boldsymbol{\phi}\;.

(111)

Due to the lack of crossings on the main fermion line,
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm DBA}
is diagonal in
\boldsymbol{\kappa}
and in
i
(but not in
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}
) namely

[\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm DBA}]^{i^{\prime}i}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\chi}}=\varphi^{ii}_{{\rm DBA},{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i)}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})\delta_{i^{\prime}i}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\;.

(112)

Component-wise in
\boldsymbol{\kappa}
, retaining the matrix structure induced by
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}=(\dots,\eta^{i-1},\eta^{i+1},\dots)
and with the dependence on
\lambda
left implicit, Eq. (
111
) reads

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\phi}^{i^{\prime}i}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\kappa})=\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\rm DBA}^{ii}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})\delta_{i^{\prime}i}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}

(113)

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\rm DBA}^{i^{\prime}i^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})\cdot\sum_{i^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime\prime}}\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{i^{\prime}i^{\prime\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime\prime})\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}^{i^{\prime\prime}i}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime\prime},\boldsymbol{\kappa})\;,

where
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\rm DBA}^{ii}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})
is the left-contracted propagator given by

\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\rm DBA}^{ii}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})=\sum_{i^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\phi}^{i^{\prime}i}_{\rm DBA}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\kappa})\;.

(114)

The steady-state population kernel can then be written as

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{K}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0)=

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{N}{\eta^{\prime}}^{j}\eta^{j}\sum_{i^{\prime}i}\langle\boldsymbol{\phi}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{-\eta^{i}}\rangle^{i^{\prime}i}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}

(115)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{N}{\eta^{\prime}}^{j}\eta^{j}\sum_{i^{\prime}i\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\phi^{i^{\prime}i}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\kappa}){\rm v}^{i}_{-\eta^{i}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})\;.

As anticipated in Sec.
V.1
, the steady-state populations
{\rm P}^{\infty}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
are the solutions of the matrix equation

\mathbf{0}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\hat{\mathcal{K}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0){\rm P}^{\infty}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\;.

(116)

VI.4
Current kernel

As shown in Sec.
V.2
, the current kernel can be calculated along the same lines as the population kernel provided that the additional constraints

\delta_{{\eta^{\prime}}^{i^{\prime}},+1}{\rm c}^{\prime}_{l}\equiv\delta_{{\eta^{\prime}}^{i^{\prime}},+1}\delta_{\alpha^{\prime},l}\delta_{\zeta^{\prime},-1}

are introduced for the last fermion line.

According to Eq. (
85
), the general formula for the steady-state current on lead
l
is

\displaystyle I^{\infty}_{l}=\;e2{\rm Re}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{I}_{l,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0){\rm P}^{\infty}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\;,

(117)

where the kernel is in Laplace space and calculated at
\lambda=0
. The current kernel formally reads

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{K}}

\displaystyle{}^{I}_{l,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0)=\prod_{j=1}^{N}{\eta^{\prime}}^{j}\eta^{j}\sum_{i^{\prime}i}\delta_{{\eta^{\prime}}^{i^{\prime}},+1}\langle{\rm c}^{\prime}_{l}\boldsymbol{\phi}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{-\eta^{i}}\rangle^{i^{\prime}i}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}

(118)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{N}{\eta^{\prime}}^{j}\eta^{j}\sum_{i^{\prime}i\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\delta_{{\eta^{\prime}}^{i^{\prime}},+1}{\rm c}^{\prime}_{l}\phi^{i^{\prime}i}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\kappa}){\rm v}^{i}_{-\eta^{i}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})\;,

cf. Eq. (
115
).

Summarizing, the steady-state populations and currents of the
N
-state system coupled to multiple leads can be obtained via Eqs. (
116
) and (
117
), respectively. Both the population and the current kernels are in turn directly given by the irreducible propagator
\boldsymbol{\phi}
which is calculated via the Dyson equations (
110
) and (
111
).

VII
Proportional coupling and connection with the Green’s functions

Consider the situation in which the central system is connected to two leads (
L
and
R
) and the tunneling amplitudes are related by

|{\rm t}_{iR\sigma}(\epsilon)|^{2}=\gamma_{iR}|{\rm t}_{iL\sigma}(\epsilon)|^{2}/\gamma_{iL}

with
\gamma_{iR}+\gamma_{iL}=1
(proportional coupling).
Current conservation at the steady state
I^{\infty}=I_{L}^{\infty}=-I_{R}^{\infty}
implies, for proportional coupling,
I^{\infty}=\sum_{i}[\gamma_{iR}I_{iL}^{\infty}-\gamma_{iL}I_{iR}^{\infty}]
, where
I_{i^{\prime}\alpha}^{\infty}=e2{\rm Re}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{I}_{i^{\prime}l,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0){\rm P}^{\infty}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
is the state-resolved steady-state current and
\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{I}_{i^{\prime}l,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0)
is given by Eq. (
118
) without the sum over the final state
i^{\prime}
. Correspondingly, we introduce the current kernel

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{I}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0):=

\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime}}\left[\gamma_{i^{\prime}R}\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{I}_{i^{\prime}L,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0)-\gamma_{i^{\prime}L}\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{I}_{i^{\prime}R,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0)\right]\;,

(119)

with the steady-state current obtained as

\displaystyle I^{\infty}=\;e2{\rm Re}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{I}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0){\rm P}^{\infty}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\;.

(120)

Consider now the Dyson equation for
\boldsymbol{\phi}
, Eq. (
113
). To obtain the current kernel for the current on lead
l
we make a contraction with the vertex as in Eq. (
118
), which gives

\displaystyle\langle{\rm c}^{\prime}_{l}\boldsymbol{\phi}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{-\eta^{i}}\rangle=

\displaystyle\langle{\rm c}^{\prime}_{l}\left[\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm DBA}+\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm DBA}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{X}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}\right]\cdot\mathbf{v}_{-\eta^{i}}\rangle\;,

(121)

or, in symbols,

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 78.74 62.99}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{34.34557pt}{40.33958pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}\end{gathered}\;,

(122)

where we highlighted the last fermion line which bears the current constraints of the lead
l
.
In the first term on the right-hand side, the vertex from which the last fermion line departs is the first vertex, the one explicitly appearing in the contraction. On the other hand, in the second term, this vertex is inside the dressed block with crossing of the main fermion line
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
. Using the relation
f^{\alpha}_{-\eta}(\epsilon_{k})=\delta_{\eta,+1}-\eta f^{\alpha}_{+}(\epsilon_{k})
, both these vertexes (denoted with red full dots in Eq. (
122
)) can be split as

{\rm v}_{-\eta^{i}}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})=\delta_{\eta^{i},+1}{\rm v}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})-\eta^{i}{\rm v}_{+}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})\;,

(123)

where
{\rm v}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})=-|{\rm t}_{i\alpha\sigma}(\epsilon_{k})|^{2}/\hbar^{2}
does not contain the Fermi function.
Then, we can write the dressed crossing block by singling-out this internal vertex as follows

\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{i^{\prime}i}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa})={\rm v}^{i^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{i^{\prime}i}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa})-{\rm v}_{+}^{i^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{+}^{i^{\prime}i}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa})\;.

(124)

Taking the difference in Eq. (
119
), the terms not containing the Fermi function, i.e. the ones with
{\rm v}^{i^{\prime}}
, cancel out for proportional coupling, so that from (
118
), (
121
), and (
124
) we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{I}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0)=

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{N}{\eta^{\prime}}^{j}\eta^{j}\sum_{i^{\prime}i}\delta_{{\eta^{\prime}}^{i^{\prime}},+1}\Big[\gamma_{i^{\prime}R}\langle{\rm c}^{\prime}_{L}\boldsymbol{\phi}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{-\eta^{i}}\rangle^{i^{\prime}i}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}

(125)

\displaystyle-\gamma_{i^{\prime}L}\langle{\rm c}^{\prime}_{R}\boldsymbol{\phi}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{-\eta^{i}}\rangle^{i^{\prime}i}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}\Big]

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle[\gamma_{i^{\prime}R}{\rm v}_{+}^{i^{\prime}L}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})-\gamma_{i^{\prime}L}{\rm v}_{+}^{i^{\prime}R}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})]\Omega^{i^{\prime}}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})\delta_{\zeta^{\prime},-1}\;,

with
{\rm v}_{+}^{il}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})={\rm v}_{+}^{i}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})\delta_{\alpha,l}
and

\displaystyle\Omega^{i^{\prime}}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\prod_{j=1}^{N}{\eta^{\prime}}^{j}\eta^{j}\delta_{{\eta^{\prime}}^{i^{\prime}},+1}\sum_{i}\Big[\eta^{i}\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{ii}_{\rm DBA}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})\delta_{i^{\prime}i}

(126)

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{i^{\prime}i^{\prime}}_{\rm DBA}

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})\sum_{i^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{+}^{i^{\prime}i^{\prime\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime\prime})\boldsymbol{\phi}^{i^{\prime\prime}i}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime\prime},\boldsymbol{\kappa}){\rm v}^{i}_{-\eta^{i}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})\Big]_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}^{(i^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}}\;,

where matrix multiplication with respect to the composite sojourn indexes
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}
is understood in the second line.

Using the above definitions and Appendix
E
, the stationary current with
proportional coupling
reads
I^{\infty}=-2{\rm Re}{\rm Tr}_{\rm S}[\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}]
.
From Eqs. (
120
) and (
125
)

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}_{\rm S}[\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}]=

\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{I}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(0){\rm P}^{\infty}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}

(127)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{i}\frac{\gamma_{iL}\gamma_{iR}}{2\pi\hbar^{2}}\int d\epsilon[f^{L}_{+}(\epsilon)-f^{R}_{+}(\epsilon)]\Gamma_{ii}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\zeta}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\Omega^{i}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\zeta,\epsilon){\rm P}^{\infty}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\delta_{\zeta,-1}\;,

where we used the property
\gamma_{iL}{\Gamma}_{iiR}(\epsilon)=\gamma_{iR}{\Gamma}_{iiL}(\epsilon)=\gamma_{iL}\gamma_{iR}{\Gamma}_{ii}(\epsilon)
.
On the other hand, from Eqs. (
278
) and (
281
)

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}_{\rm S}[\boldsymbol{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}]=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\frac{-{\rm i}\gamma_{iL}\gamma_{iR}}{2\pi\hbar}\int d\epsilon\left[f_{+}^{L}(\epsilon)-f_{+}^{R}(\epsilon)\right]\Gamma_{ii}(\epsilon)\mathcal{G}^{a}_{ii}(\epsilon)\;,

(128)

where
\mathcal{G}^{a}
is the advanced Green’s function of the central system.
Note that the blip index
\zeta
always multiplies the imaginary unit, as can be seen in the definition of the correlation functions
{\rm g}^{\zeta}
which enter the diagrammatic contributions to the propagator, see Eqs (
48
)-(
55
), along with the phase factors
{\rm b}_{kl}
defined Eq. (
42
). This means that, in the time domain,
\zeta
establishes the sign of the time variable and the real part can be obtained by removing the constraint
\delta_{\zeta,-1}
and summing over
\zeta
. We can thus make the following identification with the retarded/advanced Green’s functions

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{(\zeta)}_{ii}(\epsilon)=-

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm i}\zeta}{\hbar}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\Omega^{i}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\zeta,\epsilon){\rm P}^{\infty}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\;,

(129)

where
\zeta=+1
(
-1
) gives the retarded (advanced) Green’s function.
The exact steady state current acquires, in the continuum limit, the form of the Meir-Wingreen formula
Meir and Wingreen 1992

\displaystyle I^{\infty}=

\displaystyle\frac{e}{\hbar}\sum_{i}\int d\epsilon\left[\frac{\Gamma_{L}(\epsilon)\Gamma_{R}(\epsilon)}{\Gamma_{L}(\epsilon)+\Gamma_{R}(\epsilon)}\right]_{ii}[f^{L}_{+}(\epsilon)-f^{R}_{+}(\epsilon)]

(130)

\displaystyle\times\left[-\frac{1}{\pi}{\rm Im}\;\mathcal{G}^{r}_{ii}(\epsilon)\right]\;,

where
\Gamma_{ii\alpha}(\epsilon)=2\pi\sum_{\sigma}\varrho_{\alpha\sigma}(\epsilon)|{\rm t}_{i\alpha\sigma}(\epsilon)|^{2}
and where we used
{\rm Re}[-{\rm i}\mathcal{G}^{a}_{ii}(\epsilon)]={\rm Im}\mathcal{G}^{a}_{ii}(\epsilon)=-{\rm Im}\mathcal{G}^{r}_{ii}(\epsilon)
. The function
-(1/\pi){\rm Im}\;\mathcal{G}^{r}_{ii}(\epsilon)
is the system’s density of states in the presence of tunnel-coupling to the leads.

A quantity used to characterize the transport properties of the setup in a nonequilibrium setting, namely in the presence of a voltage bias
eV:=\mu_{L}-\mu_{R}
, is the differential conductance
\partial I/\partial V
.
At equilibrium,
\mu_{L}=\mu_{R}=\mu
, the behavior of the transport setup is described by the linear conductance
G
, defined as the limiting value
of the differential conductance for vanishing bias.
Setting
\mu_{L}=\mu+eV/2
and
\mu_{R}=\mu-eV/2
, and using
\partial f_{+}^{L}/\partial V=-(e/2)\partial f_{+}^{L}/\partial\epsilon
and
\partial f_{+}^{R}/\partial V=\;(e/2)\partial f_{+}^{R}/\partial\epsilon
, the linear conductance assumes the form
Wingreen and Meir 1994

\displaystyle G=

\displaystyle-\pi G_{0}\sum_{i}\int d\epsilon\left[\frac{\Gamma_{L}(\epsilon)\Gamma_{R}(\epsilon)}{\Gamma_{L}(\epsilon)+\Gamma_{R}(\epsilon)}\right]_{ii}\frac{\partial f_{+}(\epsilon)}{\partial\epsilon}

(131)

\displaystyle\times\left[-\frac{1}{\pi}{\rm Im}\;\mathcal{G}^{r}_{ii}(\epsilon)\right]\;,

where, due to the vanishing bias,
f_{+}^{L}(\epsilon)=f_{+}^{R}(\epsilon)=f_{+}(\epsilon)
. Here,
G_{0}:=2e^{2}/h
is the conductance quantum.
At
T=0
, the derivative of the Fermi function is
-\delta(\epsilon-\mu)
so that

\displaystyle G_{T=0}=\pi G_{0}\sum_{i}\left[\frac{\Gamma_{L}(\mu)\Gamma_{R}(\mu)}{\Gamma_{L}(\mu)+\Gamma_{R}(\mu)}\right]_{ii}\left[-\frac{1}{\pi}{\rm Im}\;\mathcal{G}^{r}_{ii}(\mu)\right]\;.

(132)

In the following, we shall apply the general formalism developed here to two archetypal models, the resonant level model and the SIAM.

VIII
Resonant level model

Up to here, we have considered a general interacting central system connected to a number of noninteracting leads, with possibly energy- and state-dependent tunnel coupling. The only constraint has been given on the correlation matrices in the form of Eq. (
29
). In Sec.
VII
, we have specialized the discussion to the case of two leads and proportional coupling.

To exemplify the construction carried out so far, we consider in this section the resonant level model (RLM). This model describes a single, spinless level of energy
\epsilon_{0}
coupled to two noninteracting leads as shown in Fig.
15
.

Figure 15:
Scheme of a spinless level of energy
\epsilon_{0}
tunnel-coupled to two noninteracting leads.

The model Hamiltonian is

\displaystyle H=

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}\;a^{\dagger}a+\sum_{\alpha k\sigma}\epsilon_{\alpha k}c^{{\dagger}}_{\alpha k\sigma}c_{\alpha k\sigma}

(133)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\alpha k\sigma}\big[{\rm t}_{\alpha k}a^{{\dagger}}c_{\alpha k\sigma}+{\rm t}^{*}_{\alpha k}c^{{\dagger}}_{\alpha k\sigma}a\big]\;.

Due to the lack of interactions in the dot, this model admits an exact solution and has been analyzed with a variety of methods in Ref.
Reimer et al. 2019
, see also
Ferguson et al. 2020
. According to the diagrammatic rules set up in Sec.
IV.4
, the contributing diagrams for a noninteracting system can have at most two overlapping fermion lines for the same electron state. Since the dot is equipped with a single state, the RTA, discussed in Sec.
VI.2
, provides the exact description of the resonant level. Hence, the exact equation (
111
) reduces to the RTA, where the crossing block
{\rm X}
is not dressed by internal processes and the dressed propagator
\phi_{\rm B}
is given by the bare propagator
\rm h
dressed by the bubbles
{\rm B}
, see Fig.
16
.

\boldsymbol{\kappa}
\boldsymbol{\kappa}
\mathbf{h}

\boldsymbol{\kappa}
\boldsymbol{\kappa}
\eta
\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{1}
\mathbf{B}

\boldsymbol{\kappa}
\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}
\eta
\mathbf{X}

Figure 16:
Blocks involved in the propagator of the resonant level model. Full dots represent the vertexes defined in Eqs. (
91
) and (
92
). Dashed and solid lines at the bottom denote blip and sojourn states, respectively. The sojourn index
\eta
assumes the values
+1
and
-1
for occupied and empty dot, respectively. These
internal
sojourns are summed over, the sum being included in the definitions of the blocks.

As a result, the exact Dyson equation (
113
) specializes to

\displaystyle\phi(\kappa^{\prime},\kappa)=

\displaystyle\varphi_{\rm NCA2}(\kappa)\delta_{\kappa^{\prime}\kappa}+\varphi_{\rm NCA2}(\kappa^{\prime})\sum_{\kappa^{\prime\prime}}{\rm X}(\kappa^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime\prime})\phi(\kappa^{\prime\prime},\kappa)\;,

(134)

where the matrix structure in the sojourn index
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{(i)}
is lost due the fact that there is a single electron state so that
\boldsymbol{\chi}\equiv\boldsymbol{\kappa}
with

\boldsymbol{\kappa}=(\zeta,\alpha,k)\;.

The dressed propagator
\phi_{\rm NCA2}(\kappa^{\prime},\kappa)=\varphi_{\rm NCA2}(\kappa^{\prime})\delta_{\kappa^{\prime}\kappa}
is in turn given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm NCA2}=

\displaystyle\left[\mathbf{h}^{-1}-\mathbf{B}\right]^{-1}\;,

(135)

according to Eq. (
109
).

In Laplace space the bare propagator
\mathbf{h}(\lambda=0^{+})
reads

[\mathbf{h}]_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}={\rm i}\hbar\frac{1}{\zeta(\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{0})+{\rm i}0^{+}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\;,

(136)

see Eq. (
91
).

The bubble block, the central diagram in Fig.
16
, is the contraction with a vertex of the internal fermion line (indexed with
1
) of the
free propagator with two overlapping fermion lines

\displaystyle[\mathbf{h}_{2}]_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}=\begin{gathered}\resizebox{}{1864679}{
\hbox to81.94pt{\vbox to63.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-27.45692pt\lower-8.733pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 39.37 59.06 C 63.73 73.82 93.75 73.82 118.11 59.06}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{89.68712pt}{38.95915pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}

}\end{gathered}={\rm i}\hbar\frac{\delta_{\zeta_{1},-\zeta}}{\zeta(\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{k_{1}})+{\rm i}0^{+}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\;.

(137)

Here, the index
\zeta_{1}
is constrained to be equal to
-\zeta
by the diagrammatic rules, see Eq. (
51
) where the upper indexes of the correlation functions
{\rm g}^{\zeta}_{\eta}
have opposite signs.
Note that the block
{\rm h}_{2}
bears no dependence on the dot energy as the system is in a sojourn state, denoted with
\eta
, contrary to the block
{\rm h}
in Eq. (
136
) where the system is in a blip state (dashed line, see the left diagram in Fig.

).
Including the sum over the internal sojourn
\eta
due to the sum-over-paths, the bubble block is evaluated as follows

\displaystyle[\mathbf{B}]_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}=

}\end{gathered}=\sum_{\eta}\langle{\rm h}_{2}{\rm v}_{\eta}\rangle

(138)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle i}\zeta\hbar\sum_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{1}}\frac{\sum_{\eta}{\rm v}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{1})\delta_{\zeta_{1},-\zeta}}{\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{k_{1}}+{\rm i}\zeta 0^{+}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{{\rm i}\zeta}{\hbar}\sum_{\alpha_{1},k_{1}}\frac{|{\rm t}_{\alpha_{1}}(\epsilon_{k_{1}})|^{2}}{\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{k_{1}}+{\rm i}\zeta 0^{+}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\;,

where we used
\sum_{\eta}f_{\eta}(\epsilon_{k})=1
in the vertex

{\rm v}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}):=-\frac{|{\rm t}_{\alpha}(\epsilon_{k})|^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}f^{\alpha}_{\eta}(\epsilon_{k})\;.

(139)

In the wide-band limit (WBL), i.e. for energy independent tunneling amplitudes, using the result of Eq. (
310
) we obtain

\displaystyle[\mathbf{B}]_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}=

\displaystyle\zeta\frac{\rm i}{\hbar}\sum_{\alpha_{1}}\varrho_{\alpha_{1}}|{\rm t}_{\alpha_{1}}|^{2}\int d\epsilon_{1}\;\frac{\delta_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}}{\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{k}-{\rm i}\zeta 0^{+}}

(140)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\hbar}\pi\sum_{\alpha_{1}}\varrho_{\alpha_{1}}|{\rm t}_{\alpha_{1}}|^{2}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\Gamma}{2\hbar}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\;,

where
\Gamma=2\pi\sum_{\alpha}\varrho_{\alpha}|{\rm t}_{\alpha}|^{2}
. As a result, from Eq. (
135
),

\varphi_{\rm NCA2}(\zeta,k)={\rm i}\zeta\hbar\frac{1}{\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{0}+{\rm i}\zeta\Gamma/2}\;.

(141)

The block
\mathbf{X}
is given by attaching the vertex
{\rm v}_{\eta}
to the outgoing fermion line (with index
\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}
) in the propagator

\displaystyle[\mathbf{h}^{\rm X}_{2}]_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle i}\hbar\frac{\delta_{\zeta^{\prime},-\zeta}}{\zeta(\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{k^{\prime}})+{\rm i}0^{+}}\;,

(142)

resulting in

\displaystyle[\mathbf{X}]_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}=

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\resizebox{}{1305270}{
\hbox to120.04pt{\vbox to58.27pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-5.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{99.53372pt}{36.74919pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 L 39.37 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 39.37 0 L 110.24 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
