{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{49.77025pt}{7.68884pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 110.24 0 L 129.92 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 39.37 0 M 46.29 0 C 46.29 3.82 43.19 6.92 39.37 6.92 C 35.55 6.92 32.45 3.82 32.45 0 C 32.45 -3.82 35.55 -6.92 39.37 -6.92 C 43.19 -6.92 46.29 -3.82 46.29 0 Z M 39.37 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}\end{gathered}

(143)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm i}\zeta\hbar\;\sum_{\eta}{\rm v}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})}{\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{k^{\prime}}+{\rm i}\zeta 0^{+}}\delta_{\zeta^{\prime},-\zeta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{{\rm i}\zeta}{\hbar}\frac{\;|{\rm t}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(\epsilon_{k^{\prime}})|^{2}}{\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{k^{\prime}}+{\rm i}\zeta 0^{+}}\delta_{\zeta^{\prime},-\zeta}\;.

Here, as for
\mathbf{B}
, the internal sojourn index
\eta
is summed over due to the sum-over-path with the result that
\mathbf{X}
does not contain the Fermi function.

The retarded/advanced Green’s function are given by Eq. (
129
) via the function
\Omega_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}
defined in Eq. (
126
) which, in the RLM, adapts to

\displaystyle\Omega_{{\eta}^{\prime}\eta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}\eta\delta_{\eta^{\prime},+1}\Big[\eta\varphi_{\rm NCA2}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})

(144)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\varphi_{\rm NCA2}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})\sum_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime\prime}}{\rm x}_{+}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\kappa})\phi(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime\prime}){\rm v}_{-\eta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime\prime})\Big]\;.

The block
{\rm x}_{+}
, see Eqs. (
123
)-(
124
) is easy to evaluate and from Eq. (
143
) reads

{\rm x}_{+}=\frac{{\rm i}\zeta\hbar\;\sum_{\eta}\eta}{\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{k^{\prime}}+{\rm i}\zeta 0^{+}}\delta_{\zeta^{\prime},-\zeta}=0\;.

Thus,

\Omega_{{\eta}^{\prime}\eta}(\zeta,k)=\delta_{\eta^{\prime},+1}\varphi_{\rm NCA2}(\zeta,k)\;,

and the resulting expression for the Green’s function is

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{(\zeta)}(\epsilon_{k})=

\displaystyle-\frac{{\rm i}\zeta}{\hbar}\sum_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}\Omega_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}(\zeta,k){\rm P}^{\infty}_{\eta}

(145)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{{\rm i}\zeta}{\hbar}\varphi_{\rm NCA2}(\zeta,k)\sum_{\eta}{\rm P}^{\infty}_{\eta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{0}+{\rm i}\zeta\Gamma/2}\;,

where we used Eq. (
141
). Thus, as expected, the single-particle Green’s function acquires a broadening
\Gamma/2
due to the coupling of the resonant level to the leads.

Substituting
\mathcal{G}^{(\zeta=+1)}(\epsilon)
in Eq. (
130
), the current for proportional coupling,
\gamma_{L}\Gamma_{R}=\gamma_{R}\Gamma_{L}
(with
\gamma_{L}+\gamma_{R}=1
), in the WBL reads
1
1

1

This integral can be solved by noting that the denominator in the integral splits as
\left\{[(\epsilon-\epsilon_{0})-{\rm i}\Gamma/2]^{-1}-[(\epsilon-\epsilon_{0})+{\rm i}\Gamma/2]^{-1}\right\}/{\rm i}\Gamma
and by applying Eq. (
315
).

\displaystyle I^{\infty}=

\displaystyle\frac{e}{h}\int d\epsilon\;[f^{L}_{+}(\epsilon)-f^{R}_{+}(\epsilon)]\frac{\Gamma_{L}\Gamma_{R}}{(\epsilon-\epsilon_{0})^{2}+\Gamma^{2}/4}

(146)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e}{h}\frac{\Gamma_{L}\Gamma_{R}}{\Gamma}r(\epsilon_{0})\;,

where
\Gamma=\Gamma_{L}+\Gamma_{R}
and where we defined

\displaystyle r(x)=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Im}\psi\left(\frac{1}{2}+{\rm i}\frac{x-\mu_{L}-{\rm i}\frac{\Gamma}{2}}{2\pi k_{\rm B}T}\right)

(147)

\displaystyle-{\rm Im}\psi\left(\frac{1}{2}+{\rm i}\frac{x-\mu_{R}-{\rm i}\frac{\Gamma}{2}}{2\pi k_{\rm B}T}\right)\;,

with
\Psi(x)
the digamma function. Equation (
146
) provides the exact, finite-temperature expression for the current in the RLM.

Summarizing, while the RTA gives the exact irreducible propagator
\phi
and consequently the exact density matrix and current for the RLM, the crossing diagrams do not contribute to the retarded/advanced Green’s functions (and therefore to the current for proportional coupling).
Note that, as the dot can host at most one electron, the system is necessarily noninteracting and consequently inelastic processes are absent. This gives the Landauer formula Eq. (
146
), where the temperature dependence is exclusively in the Fermi functions. Using Eq. (
131
) for the conductance, with the imaginary part of Eq. (
145
), we readily obtain the analytical expression for the conductance at
T=0
, where
-\partial f_{+}/\partial\epsilon=\delta(\epsilon-\mu)
, which is of the Breit-Wigner form
Haug and Jahuo 2008
;
Scheer and Cuevas 2010

G=\frac{2e^{2}}{h}\frac{\Gamma_{L}\Gamma_{R}/2}{(\mu-\epsilon_{0})^{2}+\Gamma^{2}/4}\;.

(148)

For
\Gamma_{\alpha}=\Gamma/2
the conductance saturates to half of the quantum of conductance
G_{0}=2e^{2}/h
at resonance, i.e. for
\mu=\epsilon_{0}
. In the noninteracting
spinful
model, where the dot can host two electrons with opposite spin, the sum over
\sigma
yields the maximum

G_{\rm max}=G_{0}
.

IX
Single-impurity Anderson model (SIAM)

Figure 17:
Single-impurity Anderson model realized by a quantum dot tunnel-coupled to two noninteracting leads.

We now specialize the discussion to the simplest, yet highly nontrivial, instance of the general model of interacting nanostructure described by Eq. (
1
), the single-impurity Anderson model (SIAM). In the SIAM, the central system is a quantum dot that can host at most two electrons with opposite spin states, the latter being denoted by
\sigma=\;\uparrow,\downarrow
. The dot is connected to two leads,
L
and
R
, via a tunnel coupling which we assume here to be spin-independent. A scheme of the model is provided in Fig.
17
. The resulting four many-body dot states are given by
|0\rangle
,
|\uparrow\rangle
,
|\downarrow\rangle
, and
|2\rangle
, or, in terms of the sojourn indexes
(\eta^{\uparrow},\eta^{\downarrow})
, by
|-1-1\rangle
,
|+1-1\rangle
,
|-1+1\rangle
, and
|+1+1\rangle
, respectively.
The difference in chemical potentials produces at long times a stationary current which is essentially determined by three energy scales: The interaction energy
U
between the electrons in the dot, the tunnel coupling
\Gamma
, and the thermal energy
k_{\rm B}T
.
Despite its simplicity, this model already displays a variety of interesting physical effects and transport regimes arising from the interplay of these energy scales, see Fig.
18
below. For example, the Kondo effect
Hewson 1993
;
van der Wiel et al. 2000
;
Kouwenhoven and Glazman 2001
, an exquisitely nonperturbative many-body phenomenon due to the correlation between electrons in the dot and the leads, becomes relevant for temperatures around and below a certain value
T_{\rm K}
which depends exclusively on
\Gamma
,
U
, and the single-particle energies
\epsilon_{\sigma}
.

The full Hamiltonian of the setup reads

\displaystyle H=

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma}\epsilon_{\sigma}\hat{n}_{\sigma}+U\hat{n}_{\uparrow}\hat{n}_{\downarrow}+\sum_{\alpha k\sigma}\epsilon_{\alpha k}c^{{\dagger}}_{\alpha k\sigma}c_{\alpha k\sigma}

(149)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\alpha k\sigma}\big[{\rm t}_{\alpha k}a_{\sigma}^{{\dagger}}c_{\alpha k\sigma}+{\rm t}^{*}_{\alpha k}c^{{\dagger}}_{\alpha k\sigma}a_{\sigma}\big]\;,

where
\alpha=L,R
, and
\hat{n}_{\sigma}=a_{\sigma}^{{\dagger}}a_{\sigma}
. The single-particle energies
\epsilon_{\sigma}
are possibly split by an externally applied magnetic field,
\epsilon_{\uparrow}-\epsilon_{\downarrow}=\Delta_{B}
. Note that spin-independent coupling constants
\rm t_{\alpha k}
(and non-magnetic leads) imply that the correlation matrices
\mathbf{g}_{\pm}
, Eq. (
7
), are not only diagonal, but also proportional to the identity, namely
[\mathbf{g}_{\pm}(t)]_{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}=\delta_{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}\;{\rm g}_{\pm}(t)
with

{\rm g}_{\pm}(t)=\frac{1}{\hbar^{2}}\sum_{\alpha k}|{\rm t}_{\alpha k}|^{2}f_{\pm}^{\alpha}(\epsilon_{k})e^{-\frac{\rm i}{\hbar}\epsilon_{\alpha k}t}\;.

(150)

In what follows we use the compact notation of Eq. (
22
) which, in the case considered here, adapts to

\displaystyle{\rm g}_{+1}^{+1}

\displaystyle={\rm g}_{+},\quad{\rm g}_{+1}^{-1}={\rm g}_{+}^{*},\quad{\rm g}_{-1}^{+1}={\rm g}_{-},\quad{\rm g}_{-1}^{-1}={\rm g}_{-}^{*}\;.

(151)

In the WBL, the tunnel coupling is quantified by
\Gamma:=2\pi\sum_{\alpha}\varrho_{\alpha}|{\rm t}_{\alpha}|^{2}
.
The collective index
\boldsymbol{\chi}
, defined for the general case in Eq. (
86
), specializes in the SIAM to

\boldsymbol{\chi}:=(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\eta^{\bar{\sigma}}),\quad{\rm where}\quad\boldsymbol{\kappa}=(\zeta,\alpha,k)

and where
\bar{\sigma}
denotes the opposite spin with respect to
\sigma
.
In order to simplify the notation, from here on we make the identifications

\eta^{\sigma}\equiv\nu\quad{\rm and}\quad\eta^{\bar{\sigma}}\equiv\eta\;,

with
\bar{\nu}\equiv-\nu
and
\bar{\eta}\equiv-\eta
, so that
\boldsymbol{\chi}:=(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\eta)
.
The system energies
E_{i}
in the phase factors of Eq. (
42
)
are in this case the dot energies shown in Fig.
7
. For the fermion lines associated to the state
\sigma
these energies read

\displaystyle

\displaystyle E_{\sigma}=\epsilon_{\sigma}+U/2\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad\bar{\sigma}\text{ in a blip state}

(152)

\displaystyle E_{\sigma}(\eta)=\epsilon_{\sigma}+(1+\eta)U/2\;\;\qquad\bar{\sigma}\text{ in the sojourn $\eta$}\;,

with similar expressions for
E_{\bar{\sigma}}
and
E_{\bar{\sigma}}(\nu)
.

The retarded (
\zeta=+1
) and advanced (
\zeta=-1
) dot Green’s functions, Eq. (
129
), adopt in the SIAM the following form

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{(\zeta)}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon_{k})=-

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm i}\zeta}{\hbar}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\Omega^{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\zeta,k){\rm P}^{\infty}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\;,

(153)

with

\displaystyle\Omega^{\sigma}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\eta}}

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\kappa})=\eta^{\prime}\eta\delta_{\nu^{\prime},+1}\sum_{s=\uparrow,\downarrow}\Big[\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\rm B}^{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})\delta_{\sigma s}+\nu\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\rm B}^{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})

(154)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{+}^{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime\prime})\boldsymbol{\phi}^{\sigma^{\prime\prime}s}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime\prime},\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}){\rm v}_{-\eta^{s}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})\Big]_{\eta^{\prime}\eta^{\bar{s}}}\;,

where we used
(\nu)^{2}=1
.
Here, the blocks in parenthesis have a
2\times 2
matrix structure induced by the sojourn indexes
\eta^{\prime}\eta
, and the vertex is given by

{\rm v}_{-\eta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}):=-\frac{|{\rm t}_{\alpha}(\epsilon_{k})|^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}f^{\alpha}_{-\eta}(\epsilon_{k})\;.

(155)

The Meir-Wingreen formula, which gives the current for a general system in the case of proportional coupling with the leads, Eq. (
130
), adapts for the SIAM to

\displaystyle I^{\infty}=

\displaystyle\frac{e}{\hbar}\frac{\Gamma_{L}\Gamma_{R}}{\Gamma}\sum_{\sigma}\int d\epsilon\;[f^{L}_{+}(\epsilon)-f^{R}_{+}(\epsilon)]\left[-\frac{1}{\pi}{\rm Im}\mathcal{G}^{r}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon)\right]\;.

(156)

The asymptotic population of the spin states
\sigma
is the trace over the occupation states of
\bar{\sigma}
of the SIAM populations
{\rm P}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\infty}
, namely
{\rm p}^{\sigma}_{\nu}=\sum_{\eta}{\rm P}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\infty}
. In terms of the expectation value of the number operator
\hat{n}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle{\rm p}^{\sigma}_{+}=\langle\hat{n}_{\sigma}\rangle\;,\qquad{\rm p}^{\sigma}_{-}=1-\langle\hat{n}_{\sigma}\rangle\;.

(157)

These expectation values can be calculated either by solving the master equation for
{\rm P}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\infty}
, see Eq. (
116
), or self-consistently, via the equations of motion technique
Lavagna 2015
, where, in the wide-band limit,

\displaystyle\langle\hat{n}_{\sigma}\rangle=\frac{1}{\Gamma}\int d\epsilon\left[\Gamma_{L}f_{+}^{L}(\epsilon)+\Gamma_{R}f_{+}^{R}(\epsilon)\right]\left[-\frac{1}{\pi}{\rm Im}\mathcal{G}^{r}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon)\right]\;,

(158)

with
\Gamma=\Gamma_{L}+\Gamma_{R}
.

IX.1
Approximation schemes

Despite the existing rich literature on the SIAM, an exact analytical solution for the dot Green’s function, Eq. (
153
), encompassing the whole regime of parameters
U
,
\Gamma
, and
T
, is not known so far. In the forthcoming sections, we show how known perturbative schemes in
\Gamma
, as well as some nonperturbative ones, are recovered within our approach. Furthermore, a novel infinite-tier approximation scheme is discussed.

In Fig.
18
we sketch the regime of validity of different analytical approaches derived from the diagrammatic unravelling of the exact irreducible propagator in Eq. (
98
). The sequential tunneling and cotunneling schemes are perturbative in
\Gamma
and thus valid when
\Gamma
is the smallest energy scale, namely
\Gamma\ll k_{\rm B}T
for
U=0
and
\Gamma\ll k_{\rm B}T,U
for
U\neq 0
. In order to access the regime
\Gamma\sim k_{\rm B}T
one needs to include processes of all orders. The simplest way to do this is to truncate the hierarchy of diagrams discussed in Sec.
VI.2
to a maximum overlap of two fermion lines. We call the resulting schemes second-tier. Iterating the insertion of the bare bubble and crossing blocks defined above in the bare propagator
\mathbf{h}
and summing the geometrical series results in the RTA
J. König et al. 1996
;
O. Karlström et al. 2013
, where the propagator
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm RTA}
is the solution of the Dyson equation (
106
). Neglecting the crossing blocks of the RTA, one is left with a main fermion line dressed by bubbles, a scheme which we call NCA2. It generalizes the dressed second order (DSO), which accounts only for charge fluctuations internal to the main fermion line
J. Kern and M.
Grifoni 2013
. In our language the DSO considers the bubble blocks diagonal in the index
\eta
. On the contrary, the
NCA2 takes into account the full matrix structure of the bubbles and, along with the RTA, recovers the noninteracting Green’s functions.

T
0
T_{\rm K}
\Gamma
CT, ST  (pert.)
RTA, NCA2, DSO (
2
nd-tier)
NCA4, sNCA4 (
4
th-tier)
DBA, NCA (
\infty
-tier)

Figure 18:
The different approximation schemes for the SIAM discussed in this work, in their range of validity (
k_{\rm B}=1
). Dashed lines indicate the expected regime of validity. Sequential tunneling (ST) and cotunneling (CT) are perturbative in
\Gamma
(first and second order, respectively). The second-tier noncrossing approximation (NCA2) and the resonant tunneling approximation (RTA) are nonperturbative in
\Gamma
and neglect diagrams with overlap of more than two fermion lines (second-tier schemes). The first is obtained from the second by neglecting the crossing diagrams and yields the Meir-Wingreen-Lee result
Meir et al. 1991
for the retarded Green’s function. Higher-tier approximation schemes deepen the hierarchy of fermion lines. We propose novel infinite- and fourth-tier schemes which neglect the crossings at the first (DBA) or at all levels (NCA, NCA4, sNCA4) and can be seen as dressed versions of the NCA2.

These second-tier schemes display artifacts such as the pinning of the density of states at the particle-hole symmetry point, due to the temperature-independent self-energy at this symmetry point, and do not predict the correct Kondo temperature
T_{\rm K}
. Crossing diagrams contribute to inelastic processes but are not expected to be relevant for investigating the zero-bias anomaly. For these reasons, in Sec.
IX.4
, we discuss the infinite-tier approximation DBA which allows to recover the NCA2 form for the Green’s function but with dressed self-energies, see Eqs. (
210
) and (
215
) below. In Sec.
IX.6
, we explicitly evaluate such self-energies in a simplified version of the fourth-tier scheme NCA4 (sNCA4). In concluding this sub-section, we notice that the NCA scheme discussed here is different from the slave-boson NCA for the SIAM reviewed in
Bickers1987
. We nevertheless use the same name since we similarly neglect the crossing diagrams at all levels.

IX.2
Perturbative schemes

IX.2.1
Sequential tunneling

The simplest approximation, valid for
k_{\rm B}T,U\gg\Gamma
(or
k_{\rm B}T\gg\Gamma
if
U=0
), consists in truncating to the lowest order in
\Gamma
the Dyson equation for the propagator
\boldsymbol{\phi}
, Eq. (
103
). This results in
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm ST}=\mathbf{h}
, where the bare propagator with a single fermion line reads for the SIAM
[\mathbf{h}]^{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\chi}}={\rm h}^{\sigma}_{\eta\eta}\delta_{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\chi}}
, where

\displaystyle{\rm h}^{\sigma}_{\eta\eta}=

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\resizebox{}{2237610}{
\hbox to99.62pt{\vbox to74.73pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-13.23055pt\lower-24.04062pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 51.18 C 50.14 68.76 87.66 68.76 118.11 51.18}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{89.68712pt}{33.26868pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 L 118.11 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{89.68712pt}{-16.67484pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}\end{gathered}=\;{\rm i}\hbar\frac{1}{\zeta[\epsilon_{k}-E_{\sigma}(\eta)]+{\rm i}0^{+}}\;.

(159)

This propagator yields the current to the lowest order, namely the second order in the tunneling amplitude
{\rm t}
(or first order in
\Gamma
). Note that the propagator
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm ST}
is diagonal both in the spin and in the remaining variables
\boldsymbol{\chi}
. Using Eqs. (
153
) and (
154
), the Green’s functions in the ST approximation is

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{(\zeta)}_{\sigma\sigma,{\rm ST}}(\epsilon_{k})=

\displaystyle-\frac{{\rm i}\zeta}{\hbar}\sum_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}\eta^{\prime}\eta\varphi^{\sigma\sigma}_{{\rm ST},\eta^{\prime}\eta}(\zeta,k){\rm p}^{\bar{\sigma}}_{\eta}

(160)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\eta}\frac{{\rm p}^{\bar{\sigma}}_{\eta}}{\epsilon_{k}-E_{\sigma}(\eta)+{\rm i}\zeta 0^{+}}\;,

where
{\rm p}^{\bar{\sigma}}_{\eta}=\sum_{\nu}{\rm P}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\infty}
. Recall that
\varphi^{\sigma\sigma}_{{\rm ST},\eta^{\prime}\eta}(\kappa)=\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\kappa^{\prime}}\phi^{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}_{{\rm ST},\eta^{\prime}\eta}(\kappa^{\prime},\kappa)
, see Eq. (
114
).

Figure 19:
Differential conductance
vs
the gate voltage
V_{g}
and bias voltage
V
in the sequential tunneling (ST) approximation.
At low bias voltages, the current is strongly suppressed inside the regions enclosed by so-called Coulomb diamonds due to charging effects. The ST approximation does not account for virtual processes (of higher order in
\Gamma
) which enable transport of charge also inside the Coulomb diamonds.
Degenerate case,
\epsilon_{\sigma}=\epsilon_{0}=-U/2+eV_{\rm g}
, with temperature
k_{\rm B}T=0.1~U
and tunneling rates
\Gamma_{L}=\Gamma_{R}=0.005~U
.

In the ST approximation, from Eqs. (
157
) and (
160
),

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\pi}{\rm Im}\mathcal{G}^{r}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon)=

\displaystyle\delta(\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma})(1-\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle)+\delta(\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-U)\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle\;,

(161)

where we have used
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\varepsilon/(x^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})=\pi\delta(x)
,
and the general formula (
156
) gives for the curent

\displaystyle I_{\rm ST}^{\infty}=

\displaystyle\frac{e}{\hbar}\frac{\Gamma_{L}\Gamma_{R}}{\Gamma}\sum_{\sigma}\Big\{[f^{L}_{+}(\epsilon_{\sigma})-f^{R}_{+}(\epsilon_{\sigma})](1-\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle)

(162)

\displaystyle+[f^{L}_{+}(\epsilon_{\sigma}+U)-f^{R}_{+}(\epsilon_{\sigma}+U)]\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle\Big\}\;.

The levels’s populations are obtained by solving Eq. (
158
) which yields in this case

\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle=\frac{\sum_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\alpha}f_{+}^{\alpha}(\epsilon_{\sigma})}{\Gamma+\sum_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\alpha}f_{+}^{\alpha}(\epsilon_{\sigma})-\sum_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\alpha}f_{+}^{\alpha}(\epsilon_{\sigma}+U)}\;.

(163)

In Fig.
19
, we show the differential conductance
\partial I_{\rm ST}^{\infty}/\partial V
in the degenerate case, obtained from Eqs. (
162
) and (
163
) by using
\partial f_{+}^{L}/\partial V=-(e/2)\partial f_{+}^{L}/\partial\epsilon
and
\partial f_{+}^{R}/\partial V=\;(e/2)\partial f_{+}^{R}/\partial\epsilon
. The differential conductance is shown as a function of the bias voltage
eV=\mu_{L}-\mu_{R}
, and the gate voltage, which shifts the position of the (degenerate) level via
\epsilon_{0}=-U/2+eV_{\rm g}
. Such plot, called stability diagram, highlights the resonances which form diamond-shaped regions where the differential conductance is zero. In the central regions called Coulomb diamonds, the dot populations are 0, 1, and 2, from left to right, and the current is suppressed. This effect is called
Coulomb blockade and appears in the present regime of weak tunnel coupling , where the Coulomb interaction dominates and
\Gamma/k_{\rm B}T\ll 1
.

A horizontal cut (
V=0
) of the stability diagram gives the linear conductance
G=\partial I/\partial V|_{V=0}
, see Eq. (
131
). The linear conductance, which is suppressed at the center of the Coulomb diamond, shows two peaks separated by the energy
U
, see Fig.
20
below.
A straightforward extension of the ST which accounts for a
\Gamma
-broadening in the Green’s function is discussed in Sec.
IX.3.4
below.

IX.2.2
Cotunneling

The next improvement over the ST, also perturbative in
\Gamma
, is the cotunneling approximation. It allows charge transfer across the dot also in the parameter regimes where ST processes are exponentially suppressed due to the Coulomb blockade
Koller et al. 2010
, i.e. around the center of the Coulomb diamond,
V_{\rm g}\sim 0
, see Fig.
19
. In our diagrammatic approach, this occurs via virtual processes encoded in the bubble and crossing blocks
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{X}
, i.e., according to Eq. (
105
),

\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm CT}=\mathbf{h}+\mathbf{h}(\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{X})\mathbf{h}\;.

(164)

As the sequential tunneling approximation, also this scheme is valid when
\Gamma
is the smallest energy scale of the problem.

As already noticed in the case of the RLM, the diagrammatic rules in Sec.
IV.4

and in Appendix
H
imply that when there are at most two overlapping fermion lines associated to the same state (in the present case to the same spin state), the fermion line of a bubble has the index
\zeta
opposite to the one of the main fermion line. Similarly, in a crossing, the outgoing line has the index
\zeta
opposite to the one of the incoming line. These constraints do not apply when the spin states involved in a bubble or in a crossing block are different.
It is therefore natural to distinguish these two cases.

The bubble block is obtained by contracting with a vertex the internal fermion line (indexed with
1
) of the bare propagator with two overlapping fermion lines which can be of the same [
\sigma(\sigma)
], see Eq. (
165
), or of opposite [
\sigma(\bar{\sigma})
] spin.
The first is given by
[\mathbf{h}_{2}^{(\sigma)}]^{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\chi},\nu^{\prime}\nu}={\rm h}_{2}^{\sigma(\sigma)}\delta_{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}
, where

\displaystyle{\rm h}_{2}^{\sigma(\sigma)}=\begin{gathered}\resizebox{}{2424089}{

(165)

(cf. Eq. (

)).
Note that the block
\mathbf{h}_{2}^{(\sigma)}
has a

structure in the composite index

.
The propagator with overlap of two fermion lines with opposite spin is given by the block
[\mathbf{h}_{2}^{(\bar{\sigma})}]^{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}={\rm h}_{2}^{\sigma(\bar{\sigma})}\delta_{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}
, with

{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 -27.56 L 118.11 -27.56}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

}\end{gathered}={\rm i}\hbar\frac{1}{\zeta(\epsilon_{k}-E_{\sigma})+\zeta_{1}(\epsilon_{k_{1}}-E_{\bar{\sigma}})+{\rm i}0^{+}}

(166)

which has no structure in the sojourn indexes.
The bubble block resulting from the contraction of the internal fermion lines of the two above propagators with the vertexes

{\rm v}_{\pm\eta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}):=-\frac{|{\rm t}_{\alpha}(\epsilon_{k})|^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}f^{\alpha}_{\pm\eta}(\epsilon_{k})\;,

(167)

is given by

[\mathbf{B}]^{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\chi}}={\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}\delta_{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}
. The function
{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}
is the sum

\displaystyle{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle B}^{\sigma(\sigma)}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}+{\rm B}^{\sigma(\bar{\sigma})}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}

(168)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{1}}\frac{{\rm i}\hbar\sum_{\nu}{\rm v}_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{1})\delta_{\zeta_{1},-\zeta}}{\zeta(\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{k_{1}})+{\rm i}0^{+}}\delta_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{1}}\frac{{\rm i}\hbar\;{\rm v}_{-\eta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{1})}{\zeta(\epsilon_{k}-E_{\sigma})+\zeta_{1}(\epsilon_{k_{1}}-E_{\bar{\sigma}})+{\rm i}0^{+}}\;.

The first contribution, which is diagonal in all the indexes, is calculated as in Eqs. (
138
)-(
140
), and reads
{\rm B}^{\sigma(\sigma)}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}=-\Gamma/(2\hbar)\delta_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}
in the WBL.
The second term in Eq. (
168
) depends on
\eta
via the vertex and can be evaluated as well in the WBL where
\sum_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{1}}\rightarrow\sum_{\zeta_{1}\alpha_{1}}\varrho_{\alpha_{1}}\int d\epsilon_{1}
. We obtain, see Appendix
I
,

\displaystyle-\frac{\rm i}{\hbar}

\displaystyle\sum_{\zeta_{1}}\zeta_{1}\sum_{\alpha_{1}}\frac{\Gamma_{\alpha_{1}}}{2\pi}\int_{-W}^{W}d\epsilon_{1}\;\frac{f^{\alpha_{1}}_{-\eta}(\epsilon_{1})}{\epsilon_{1}-\mathcal{E}_{\zeta_{1}}+{\rm i}\zeta_{1}0^{+}}

(169)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle i}\eta\sum_{\alpha}\frac{\Gamma_{\alpha}}{2\pi\hbar}\Bigg[{\rm Re}\psi\left(\frac{1}{2}+{\rm i}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{+1}-\mu_{\alpha}}{2\pi k_{\rm B}T}\right)

\displaystyle-{\rm Re}\psi\left(\frac{1}{2}+{\rm i}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{-1}-\mu_{\alpha}}{2\pi k_{\rm B}T}\right)\Bigg]

\displaystyle-\sum_{\alpha}\frac{\Gamma_{\alpha}}{2\hbar}\left[f^{\alpha}_{-\eta}(\mathcal{E}_{+1})+f^{\alpha}_{-\eta}(\mathcal{E}_{-1})\right]\;,

where
\mathcal{E}_{\zeta_{1}}:=E_{\bar{\sigma}}+\zeta\zeta_{1}(E_{\sigma}-\epsilon)
.
All in all, the WBL expression for the bubble block reads

\displaystyle{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Gamma}{2\hbar}\delta_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}

(170)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1},\zeta_{1}}\frac{\Gamma_{\alpha_{1}}}{2\hbar}\Bigg[f^{\alpha_{1}}_{-\eta}(\mathcal{E}_{\zeta_{1}})-{\rm i}\frac{\eta\zeta_{1}}{\pi}{\rm Re}\psi\left(\frac{1}{2}+{\rm i}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\zeta_{1}}-\mu_{\alpha_{1}}}{2\pi k_{\rm B}T}\right)\Bigg]\;.

Note that the second term, while depending on the sojourn index
\eta
, is independent of
\eta^{\prime}
.
Thus, in the WBL, the matrix elements of the bubble can be written as the sum

{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}=-\frac{\Gamma}{2\hbar}\delta_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}+{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\eta}\;.

(171)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{89.68712pt}{-16.67484pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 39.37 0 M 44.9 0 C 44.9 3.06 42.43 5.53 39.37 5.53 C 36.31 5.53 33.84 3.06 33.84 0 C 33.84 -3.06 36.31 -5.53 39.37 -5.53 C 42.43 -5.53 44.9 -3.06 44.9 0 Z M 39.37 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}\end{gathered}=\frac{{\rm i}\hbar\;\sum_{\nu}{\rm v}_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})}{\zeta(\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{k^{\prime}})+{\rm i}0^{+}}\delta_{\zeta^{\prime},-\zeta}\delta_{{\eta}^{\prime}\eta}\;,

(172)

\displaystyle[\mathbf{X}]^{\bar{\sigma}\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\chi}}=

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\resizebox{}{1864679}{
\hbox to139.46pt{\vbox to72.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 20.4593pt\lower-24.04062pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}{{}}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 51.18 C 56.09 57.22 90.8 34.35 97.22 0.08}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-17.1263pt}{34.94922pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{}}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 118.11 51.18 C 81.71 57.22 46.99 34.35 40.58 0.08}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{89.68712pt}{32.17381pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 L 98.43 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 -19.69 L 39.37 -19.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.4005pt}{-16.67484pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 39.37 -19.69 L 39.37 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 39.37 -19.69 L 118.11 -19.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 98.43 0 L 118.11 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{89.68712pt}{-7.79597pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{1,0,0}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 39.37 -19.69 M 44.9 -19.69 C 44.9 -16.63 42.43 -14.15 39.37 -14.15 C 36.31 -14.15 33.84 -16.63 33.84 -19.69 C 33.84 -22.74 36.31 -25.22 39.37 -25.22 C 42.43 -25.22 44.9 -22.74 44.9 -19.69 Z M 39.37 -19.69}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}\end{gathered}=\frac{{\rm i}\hbar\;{\rm v}_{-\eta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})}{\zeta(\epsilon_{k}-E_{\sigma})+\zeta^{\prime}(\epsilon_{k^{\prime}}-E_{\bar{\sigma}})+{\rm i}0^{+}}\;,

respectively.
Here, in the spin-diagonal crossing block, we include the sum over the internal sojourn
\nu
in the definition, as done for the bubble with overlap of same-spin fermion lines, see Eq. (
168
).

In order to write the Green’s function, we use the cotunneling irreducible propagator in Eq. (
164
). This yields

\displaystyle\Omega^{\sigma,{\rm CT}}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\eta}}

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\kappa})=\eta^{\prime}\eta\delta_{\nu^{\prime},+1}\Big\{{\rm h}^{\sigma}_{\eta\eta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})\delta_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}

(173)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\Big[\mathbf{h}^{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})\mathbf{B}^{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})\mathbf{h}^{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})]_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}}\Big[\nu\mathbf{h}^{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})\mathbf{x}_{+}^{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})\mathbf{h}^{\sigma^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}){\rm v}_{-\eta^{\sigma^{\prime}}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})\Big]_{\eta^{\prime}\eta^{\bar{\sigma}^{\prime}}}\Big\}\;,

where, according to Eqs. (
123
) and (
124
), we split the crossing block
\mathbf{X}^{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}
as

{\mathbf{X}}^{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})={\rm v}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}){\mathbf{x}}^{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})-{\rm v}_{+}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}){\mathbf{x}}_{+}^{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})\;.

(174)

Now, from inspection of Eq. (
172
) we find that
\mathbf{x}_{+}^{\sigma\sigma}=0
due to the sum over
\nu
(and in analogy to the RLM case) because nothing else depends on
\nu
.
We are then left with

\displaystyle\Omega^{\sigma,{\rm CT}}_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})=

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}\eta\delta_{\nu^{\prime},+1}\Big[{\rm h}_{\eta\eta}^{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})\delta_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}

(175)

\displaystyle+{\rm h}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}^{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}){\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}){\rm h}_{\eta\eta}^{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})

\displaystyle+\nu\sum_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}}{\rm h}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}^{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}){\rm x}_{+,\eta^{\prime}\nu}^{\sigma\bar{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}){\rm h}_{\nu\nu}^{\bar{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}){\rm v}_{-\eta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})\Big]\;,

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{+,\eta^{\prime}\nu}^{\sigma\bar{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa},\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm i}\hbar\;\eta^{\prime}}{\zeta(\epsilon_{k}-E_{\sigma})+\zeta^{\prime}(\epsilon_{k^{\prime}}-E_{\bar{\sigma}})+{\rm i}0^{+}}\;.

(176)

Using the above result and Eq. (
168
) we can cast
\Omega^{\sigma}_{\rm CT}
in the form

\displaystyle\Omega^{\sigma,{\rm CT}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})=

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}\eta\delta_{\nu^{\prime},+1}\Bigg\{{\rm h}_{\eta\eta}^{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})\delta_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}+{\rm h}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}^{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}){\rm B}^{\sigma(\sigma)}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}){\rm h}_{\eta\eta}^{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})

(177)

\displaystyle+{\rm i}\hbar\sum_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}}\frac{{\rm h}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}^{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})[{\rm h}_{\eta\eta}^{\sigma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa})+\nu\eta^{\prime}{\rm h}_{\nu\nu}^{\bar{\sigma}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})]{\rm v}_{-\eta}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})}{\zeta(\epsilon_{k}-E_{\sigma})+\zeta^{\prime}(\epsilon_{k^{\prime}}-E_{\bar{\sigma}})+{\rm i}0^{+}}\Bigg\}\;.

As a result, the Green’s function is the sum of the ST contribution, given by the first term in Eq. (
177
), plus the terms of fourth order in the tunneling amplitude (second order in
\Gamma
) and reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{(\zeta)}_{\sigma\sigma,{\rm CT}}(\epsilon_{k})=-

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm i}\zeta}{\hbar}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}\Omega^{\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta},{\rm CT}}(\zeta,k){\rm P}^{\infty}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}

(178)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{(\zeta)}_{\sigma\sigma,{\rm ST}}(\epsilon_{k})+\mathcal{G}^{(\zeta)}_{\sigma\sigma,{\rm 4th}}(\epsilon_{k})\;.

For a comprehensive diagrammatic analysis of cotunneling effects we refer to
Koller et al. 2010
. In the recent article
Rohrmeier and Donarini 2021
, interference phenomena at the cotunneling level, where one needs to go beyond the assumption of state-conserving tunneling, are discussed for interacting double quantum dots. In this work, we rather focus on nonperturbative schemes.

IX.3
Nonperturbative, second-tier schemes

IX.3.1
Resonant tunneling approximation (RTA)

Iterating the insertion of the cotunneling blocks
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{X}
in the bare propagator
\mathbf{h}
, one obtains the nonperturbative RTA. The Dyson equation for the irreducible propagator
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm RTA}=[\mathbf{h}^{-1}-\mathbf{B}-\mathbf{X}]^{-1}
, Eq. (
106
), can be given in terms of the NCA2 propagator
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm NCA2}=[\mathbf{h}^{-1}-\mathbf{B}]^{-1}
(see the next section), as follows

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm RTA}=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm NCA2}+\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm NCA2}{\mathbf{X}}\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm RTA}\;.

(179)

Component-wise in
\boldsymbol{\kappa}
, with the
2\times 2
matrix structure induced by the sojourn indexes
\eta^{\uparrow},\eta^{\downarrow}
left implicit, this equation reads

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm RTA}^{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\kappa})=\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\rm NCA2}^{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})\delta_{\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}}\delta_{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}

(180)

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\rm NCA2}^{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime})\cdot\sum_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime\prime}}{\mathbf{X}}^{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime\prime})\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm RTA}^{\sigma^{\prime\prime}\sigma}(\boldsymbol{\kappa}^{\prime\prime},\boldsymbol{\kappa})\;.

The RTA is equivalent to the second order von Neumann approach
O. Karlström et al. 2013
and in the noninteracting case reproduces the current for the SIAM, but not the full density matrix, contrary to the case of the RLM which is fully described by the RTA. Indeed, as discussed in Sec.
IV.4
, for
U=0
the contributing diagrams have at most four overlapping fermion lines, of which at most two with the same spin.

In the infinite-
U
limit, the RTA admits an analytical solution to be found along the lines of
J. König 1995
;
J. König et al. 1996
. In this limit, the blocks specialize to (consider
\zeta=+1
)

\displaystyle[\mathbf{h}]^{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\chi}}=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle i}\hbar\frac{1}{\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{\sigma}+{\rm i}0^{+}}\delta_{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\chi}}\delta_{\eta,-1}\;,

(181)

\displaystyle[\mathbf{X}]^{\sigma\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\chi}}=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle i}\hbar\frac{\sum_{\nu}{\rm v}_{\nu}^{\alpha^{\prime}}(k^{\prime})}{\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{k^{\prime}}+{\rm i}0^{+}}\delta_{\zeta^{\prime},-\zeta}\;,

\displaystyle[\mathbf{X}]^{\bar{\sigma}\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\chi}}=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle i}\hbar\frac{{\rm v}_{+}^{\alpha^{\prime}}(k^{\prime})}{\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{k^{\prime}}+{\rm i}0^{+}}\delta_{\zeta^{\prime},-\zeta}\;,

\displaystyle[\mathbf{B}]^{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\chi}}=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle i}\hbar\sum_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}k^{\prime\prime}}\frac{\sum_{\nu}{\rm v}_{\nu}^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}(k^{\prime\prime})+{\rm v}_{-\eta}^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}(k^{\prime\prime})}{\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{k^{\prime\prime}}+{\rm i}0^{+}}\delta_{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\chi}}\;.

The
2\times 2
matrix structure of Eq. (
180
) is lost because
\eta
can only assume the value
-1
, as induced by the interaction energy appearing at the denominator of
{\rm h}
for
\eta=+1
, namely the dot can be occupied at most by one electron. As a result, we get the following two equations for the diagonal and off-diagonal elements in the spin index (we omit the labels RTA and NCA2)

\displaystyle\phi^{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\varphi^{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon)\delta(\epsilon-\epsilon^{\prime})

(182)

\displaystyle+\varphi^{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon)\int d\epsilon^{\prime\prime}{\rm X}^{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime\prime})\phi^{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon^{\prime\prime},\epsilon)

\displaystyle+\varphi^{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon)\int d\epsilon^{\prime\prime}{\rm X}^{\sigma\bar{\sigma}}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime\prime})\phi^{\bar{\sigma}\sigma}(\epsilon^{\prime\prime},\epsilon^{\prime})\;,

\displaystyle\phi^{\bar{\sigma}\sigma}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\varphi^{\bar{\sigma}\bar{\sigma}}(\epsilon)\int d\epsilon^{\prime\prime}{\rm X}^{\bar{\sigma}\sigma}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime\prime})\phi^{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon^{\prime\prime},\epsilon)

\displaystyle+\varphi^{\bar{\sigma}\bar{\sigma}}(\epsilon)\int d\epsilon^{\prime\prime}{\rm X}^{\bar{\sigma}\bar{\sigma}}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime\prime})\phi^{\bar{\sigma}\sigma}(\epsilon^{\prime\prime},\epsilon)\;,

where
{\rm X}^{\bar{\sigma}\bar{\sigma}}={\rm X}^{\sigma\sigma}
and
{\rm X}^{\bar{\sigma}\sigma}={\rm X}^{\sigma\bar{\sigma}}
, see Eq. (
181
).
Consider now the degenerate system,
\epsilon_{\uparrow}=\epsilon_{\downarrow}=\epsilon_{0}
. In this case
\varphi^{\sigma\sigma}=\varphi^{\bar{\sigma}\bar{\sigma}}=\varphi^{\sigma}
and, by summing the above two equations, we obtain

\displaystyle\phi^{\sigma}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\varphi^{\sigma}(\epsilon)\delta(\epsilon-\epsilon^{\prime})+\varphi^{\sigma}(\epsilon)\int d\epsilon^{\prime\prime}{\rm X}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime\prime})\phi^{\sigma}(\epsilon^{\prime\prime},\epsilon)\;,

(183)

where

\displaystyle\phi^{\sigma}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}}\phi^{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})\;,

(184)

\displaystyle{\rm X}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle X}^{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})+{\rm X}^{\bar{\sigma}\sigma}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle i}\hbar\sum_{\alpha}\frac{2{\rm v}_{+}^{\alpha}(\epsilon)+{\rm v}_{-}^{\alpha}(\epsilon)}{\epsilon-\epsilon^{\prime}+{\rm i}0^{+}}\;,

see Eq. (
181
).
Solving Eq. (
183
), it is found that the real part of
\phi
, the one which enters the current, as in Eqs. (
117
) and (
118
), reads

{\rm Re}\;\int d\epsilon^{\prime}\phi^{\sigma}(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}){\rm v}_{-\eta}(\epsilon^{\prime})=C_{\eta}\sum_{\alpha}[2{\rm v}_{+}^{\alpha}(\epsilon)+{\rm v}_{-}^{\alpha}(\epsilon)]|\varphi(\epsilon)|^{2}\;,

(185)

where
C_{\eta}
is a constant depending on the initial vertex
{\rm v}_{-\eta}
.
As shown in
J. Kern and M.
Grifoni 2013
, in this regime of large interaction
U
, the RTA and the DSO (see Sec.
IX.3.3
) display a qualitatively similar behavior of the linear conductance, though the RTA predicts higher peak-conductance. Moreover, the two schemes share the same prediction for the zero-bias anomaly temperature scale, which is given in Eq. (
198
) below.

IX.3.2
Second-tier noncrossing approximation (NCA2)

In the NCA2, the propagator
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm NCA2}
is given by the Dyson equation (
107
). As a result, the bare ST propagator is dressed by the bubble diagrams
\mathbf{B}
, namely

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm NCA2}=

\displaystyle\left[\mathbf{h}^{-1}-{\mathbf{B}}\right]^{-1}\;.

(186)

While being diagonal in
\sigma
and
\boldsymbol{\kappa}
, the bubble blocks
\mathbf{B}
have a nontrivial
2\times 2
matrix structure in terms of the sojourn index
\eta
, see Eq. (
171
), a feature which accounts for charge transfer by processes internal to the main fermion line. As a result, they induce a
2\times 2
structure to the contracted (matrix) function
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\rm NCA2}^{\sigma\sigma}(\kappa)
, defined by
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm NCA2}^{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}(\kappa^{\prime},\kappa)=\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\rm NCA2}^{\sigma\sigma}(\kappa)\delta_{\kappa^{\prime}\kappa}\delta_{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}
.

Neglecting the crossings in the main fermion lines results in the Green’s function, see Eqs. (
153
) and (
154
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{(\zeta)}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon_{k})=

\displaystyle-\frac{{\rm i}\zeta}{\hbar}\sum_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}\eta^{\prime}\eta\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\sigma\sigma}_{{\rm NCA2},\eta^{\prime}\eta}(\zeta,k){\rm p}^{\bar{\sigma}}_{\eta}

(187)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{{\rm i}\zeta}{\hbar}\sum_{\eta}\left[\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\sigma\sigma}_{{\rm NCA2},\eta\eta}(\zeta,k)-\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\sigma\sigma}_{{\rm NCA2},\bar{\eta}\eta}(\zeta,k)\right]{\rm p}^{\bar{\sigma}}_{\eta}\;.

The matrix elements of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\rm NCA2}
, from Eq. (
186
), read

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\sigma\sigma}_{{\rm NCA2},\eta\eta}=

\displaystyle\frac{({\rm h}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\bar{\eta}})^{-1}-{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\bar{\eta}}}{[({\rm h}^{\sigma}_{\eta\eta})^{-1}-{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\eta\eta}][({\rm h}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\bar{\eta}})^{-1}-{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\bar{\eta}}]-{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\eta\bar{\eta}}{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\eta}}\;,

(188)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\sigma\sigma}_{{\rm NCA2},\bar{\eta}\eta}=

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\eta}}{[({\rm h}^{\sigma}_{\eta\eta})^{-1}-{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\eta\eta}][({\rm h}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\bar{\eta}})^{-1}-{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\bar{\eta}}]-{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\eta\bar{\eta}}{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\eta}}\;,

where
{\rm h}^{\sigma}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}
is defined in Eq. (
159
) and where the dependence on
\boldsymbol{\kappa}
of the block functions is understood.
Note that the denominators are independent of
\eta
.
Plugging these results in Eq. (
187
), with
{\rm p}^{\sigma}_{\eta}
given by Eq. (
157
), the dot Green’s function reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{(\zeta)}_{\sigma\sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-{\rm i}\zeta\hbar{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{--}+{\rm i}\zeta\hbar{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{+-}\frac{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-{\rm i}\zeta\hbar[{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{--}+{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{-+}]}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-U-{\rm i}\zeta\hbar[{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{++}+{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{+-}]}}

(189)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-U-{\rm i}\zeta\hbar{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{++}+{\rm i}\zeta\hbar{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{-+}\frac{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-U-{\rm i}\zeta\hbar[{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{++}+{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{+-}]}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-{\rm i}\zeta\hbar[{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{--}+{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{-+}]}}\;.

In the WBL, we can simplify this expression by using the result in Eq. (
171
) and the property

\sum_{\eta}{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\eta}=-\Gamma/\hbar\;,

(190)

which can be checked by inspection of Eq. (
170
). The retarded (
\zeta=+1
) Green’s function in the NCA2 reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{r}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon)=

\displaystyle\frac{1-\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}+{\rm i}\Gamma/2+{\Sigma}_{\sigma-}(\epsilon)\frac{U}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-U+{\rm i}3\Gamma/2}}

(191)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-U+{\rm i}\Gamma/2-{\Sigma}_{\sigma+}(\epsilon)\frac{U}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}+{\rm i}3\Gamma/2}}\;,

where we have singled-out the constant broadening
{\rm i}\Gamma/2
and identified the
interaction-induced contributions with the off-diagonal matrix elements of the bubbles via

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\sigma\eta}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle:={\rm i}\hbar{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\eta}(\kappa)|_{\zeta=+1}

(192)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\alpha_{1}}

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma_{\alpha_{1}}}{2\pi}\sum_{\zeta_{1}}\Bigg[\eta\zeta_{1}{\rm Re}\psi\left(\frac{1}{2}+{\rm i}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\zeta_{1}}-\mu_{\alpha_{1}}}{2\pi k_{\rm B}T}\right)+{\rm i}\pi f^{\alpha_{1}}_{-\eta}(\mathcal{E}_{\zeta_{1}})\Bigg]\;,

with
\mathcal{E}_{\zeta_{1}}:=E_{\bar{\sigma}}+\zeta_{1}(E_{\sigma}-\epsilon)
and
E_{\sigma}=\epsilon_{\sigma}+U/2
.
Note that, by virtue of the property (
190
), which implies

\Sigma_{\sigma\eta}(\epsilon)=-\Sigma_{\sigma\bar{\eta}}(\epsilon)-{\rm i}\Gamma\;,

(193)

we can express the retarded Green’s function in terms of a single self energy, e.g.
\Sigma_{\sigma-}(\epsilon)
. Interestingly, the result in Eq. (
191
) is equivalent to the one obtained by Meir, Wingreen, and Lee in
Meir et al. 1991
with the equations of motion technique.

We now summarize the properties and predictions of the NCA2.
For vanishing interaction,
U=0
, we recover the correct WBL result for the noninteracting case

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{r}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}+{\rm i}\Gamma/2}\;.

(194)

As anticipated below Eq. (
148
), the zero-temperature conductance in the noninteracting limit

G=G_{0}\frac{\Gamma_{L}\Gamma_{R}}{(\mu-\epsilon_{0})^{2}+\Gamma^{2}/4}

(195)

saturates, at resonance and for
\Gamma_{\alpha}=\Gamma/2
, to the conductance quantum
G_{0}
due to the sum over the spin degree of freedom. This can be seen by applying the general formula, Eq. (
131
), to the SIAM with the retarded Green’s function given by Eq. (
194
).

For finite interaction, on the other hand, the NCA2 displays a zero-bias anomaly at a characteristic temperature
T^{*}=T_{\rm NCA2}
.
This temperature is defined as the one at which the real parts of the denominators vanish, causing a peak in the density of states
-{\rm Im}[\mathcal{G}^{r}_{\sigma\sigma}(\mu)]/\pi
. By virtue of the property (
193
) satisfied by the self-energies, this condition is the same for the two terms in Eq. (
191
) and reads

\displaystyle(\mu-\epsilon_{0})(\mu-\epsilon_{0}-U)-3\Gamma^{2}/4+U\;{\rm Re}[\Sigma_{\sigma-}(\mu)|_{T^{*}}]=0\;.

(196)

For sufficiently large interaction energy
U
, away from the condition
\mu-\epsilon_{0}=U/2
, we can approximate the real part of the self-energy in Eq. (
192
) as

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\Sigma_{\sigma-}(\mu)|_{T^{*}}\simeq\frac{\Gamma}{2\pi}\ln\left(\frac{U}{2\pi k_{\rm B}T^{*}}\right)\;.

(197)

Then, assuming
U\gg\Gamma
, solving Eq. (
196
) for
T^{*}=T_{\rm NCA2}
, we find

\displaystyle k_{\rm B}T_{\rm NCA2}\simeq\frac{U}{2\pi}e^{2\pi\frac{(\mu-\epsilon_{0})(\mu-\epsilon_{0}-U)}{U\Gamma}}\;.

(198)

Notice that this result differs from the Kondo temperature
T_{\rm K}
, which has the prefactor
\pi
in place of
2\pi
at the exponent
van der Wiel et al. 2000
;
Kouwenhoven and Glazman 2001
, namely

\displaystyle k_{\rm B}T_{\rm K}=\;\frac{\sqrt{U\Gamma}}{2}e^{\pi\frac{(\mu-\epsilon_{0})(\mu-\epsilon_{0}-U)}{U\Gamma}}\;,

(199)

with
\lim_{U\to\infty}k_{\rm B}T_{\rm K}\propto\exp[-\pi(\mu-\epsilon_{0})/\Gamma]
.

Another problem of this approximation scheme is the temperature-independent behavior at the particle-hole symmetry point (sp)
\mu-\epsilon_{0}=U/2
at equilibrium in the degenerate case
Kashcheyevs et al. 2006
. Indeed, since
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}-\mu=\mp(\epsilon-\mu)
, we have
f_{+}(\mathcal{E}_{+})=f_{-}(\mathcal{E}_{-})
. Also
{\rm Re}\psi(x+{\rm i}y)={\rm Re}\psi(x-{\rm i}y)
. Thus, from Eq. (
192
) we find that the self-energy is purely imaginary and temperature-independent

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\sigma\eta}^{\rm sp}(\epsilon)=-{\rm i}\Gamma/2\;.

(200)

Consequently, the retarded Green’s function becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{r,{\rm sp}}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon)=

\displaystyle\frac{1-\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle}{\epsilon+U/2+{\rm i}\Gamma/2-{\rm i}(\Gamma/2)\frac{U}{\epsilon-U/2+{\rm i}3\Gamma/2}}

(201)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle}{\epsilon-U/2+{\rm i}\Gamma/2+{\rm i}(\Gamma/2)\frac{U}{\epsilon+U/2+{\rm i}3\Gamma/2}}\;.

This feature causes the onset of an artifact in the linear conductance
G
when the temperature decreases below
\Gamma
: A pinning at a temperature-independent value of
G
at the symmetry point.
Note that inclusion of the crossings does not lift this problem: This can be seen from Fig. 3 of Ref.
Pedersen and Wacker 2005
where a scheme equivalent to the RTA, which includes the crossings, is used.

The linear conductance from the NCA2 is shown in Fig.
20
, for the degenerate case, as a function of the gate voltage
\epsilon_{0}-\mu
for various temperatures. At high temperatures,
k_{\rm B}T>\Gamma
, there are two temperature-broadened peaks separated by the energy
U
, in agreement with the ST result shown in Fig.
19
. Upon decreasing
T
, the peaks get narrower and closer, witnessing the transition to
\Gamma
-broadened conductance peaks, where the dot energies are renormalized by the tunnel coupling to the leads. This is captured by the nonperturbative character of the NCA2. As anticipated above, at low temperatures,
k_{\rm B}T\ll\Gamma
, the pinning of
G
at a temperature-independent value appears at the particle-hole symmetry point
\epsilon_{0}-\mu=-U/2
. The onset of this artifact signals the breakdown of the approximation scheme.
As shown in Sec.
IX.6
, introducing higher-tier processes which dress the NCA2 bubble diagrams, this problem is lifted as the dependence of the self-energies on the temperature is restored.

Figure 20:
Linear conductance within the NCA2, in units of the conductance quantum
G_{0}=2e^{2}/h
,
vs
the gate voltage
\epsilon_{0}-\mu
in the degenerate case
\epsilon_{\sigma}=\epsilon_{0}
.
The peaks shrink and move towards the center as temperature is decreased. The curves at the lowest temperatures display an unphysical pinning at the particle-hole symmetry point.
The tunnel coupling is
\Gamma=0.1~U
with
\Gamma_{L}=\Gamma_{R}=\Gamma/2
.

IX.3.3
Charge fluctuations only: Dressed second order

Figure 21:
Linear conductance within the NCA2 and the DSO schemes compared with the curves from the numerically-exact DM-NRG. We consider the degenerate case
\epsilon_{\sigma}=\epsilon_{0}
for three different temperatures. The tunnel coupling is
\Gamma=0.2~U
with
\Gamma_{L}=\Gamma_{R}=\Gamma/2
. The parameters are chosen to allow a direct comparison with the results of
de Souza Melo et al. 2019
.

The DSO is the simplest, nontrivial approximation scheme nonperturbative in
\Gamma
, being the dressed version of the sequential tunneling, see Eq. (
104
), where the main fermion line is dressed by charge fluctuations
Koller et al. 2012
;
J. Kern and M.
Grifoni 2013
;
Dirnaichner et al. 2015
. The diagrams retained are formally similar to the ones of the NCA2, namely they consist in dressing the main fermion lines with bare bubble diagrams. The difference is that the DSO only accounts for charge fluctuations of the main fermion line, meaning that the sojourns states before and after a bubble are the same,
\eta=\eta_{1}=\dots=\eta^{\prime}
. As a consequence, the charge in the dot does not vary by more than one unit between the two ends of the main fermion line, net charge transfers being operated solely by the latter. In this scheme, the kernel connects the same states as those connected by the sequential tunneling, the states that differ by no more than one electron in occupancy. Note that the same is not true for the NCA2, which includes also processes that vary the dot charge also within the main line (pair tunneling).
The fact that the internal processes leave the intermediate sojourns unchanged means that the bubbles in the Dyson equation (
208
) for the function
\varphi^{\sigma\sigma}
have a diagonal structure yielding the solution

\displaystyle\varphi^{\sigma\sigma}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta,{\rm DSO}}(\kappa)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{({\rm h}^{\sigma}_{\eta\eta})^{-1}-{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\eta\eta}}\delta_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}\;.

(202)

Thus, according to Eq. (
187
), the retarded DSO Green’s function assumes, in the WBL, the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{r}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon)=

\displaystyle\frac{1-\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}+{\rm i}\Gamma/2-\Sigma_{\sigma-}(\epsilon)}

(203)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-U+{\rm i}\Gamma/2-\Sigma_{\sigma+}(\epsilon)}\;,

with
\Sigma_{\sigma\eta}(\epsilon)
given by Eq. (
192
).
Note that, contrary to the NCA2, the correct result in the noninteracting limit is
not
recovered within the DSO.
However, in the limit
U\rightarrow\infty
, the DSO reproduces the NCA2 result in the same limit, namely one obtains

\displaystyle\lim_{U\to\infty}\mathcal{G}^{r}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon)=

\displaystyle\frac{1-\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}+{\rm i}\Gamma/2-\Sigma_{\sigma-}(\epsilon)}\;.

(204)

The corresponding prediction for the Kondo-like temperature
T_{\rm DSO}
is in this limit

k_{\rm B}T_{\rm DSO}\simeq\frac{U}{2\pi}e^{-2\pi(\mu-\epsilon_{0})/\Gamma}\;,

(205)

and is the same as for the NCA2 and RTA, with the wrong prefactor in the exponent.
Nevertheless, the DSO is the simplest scheme, namely the one with the minimal collection of diagrams, which captures the emergence of a zero-bias anomaly at low temperatures,
k_{\rm B}T\leq\Gamma
.
A comparison between the linear conductance calculated within the NCA2 and the one from the DSO is shown in Fig.
21
, for
k_{\rm B}T/\Gamma=0.2
, where the linear conductance follows qualitatively the DM-NRG result, down to
k_{\rm B}T/\Gamma=0.002
, where both schemes break down. The parameters are chosen so as to allow for a direct comparison with Fig. 6 of Ref.
de Souza Melo et al. 2019
, where the scheme EOM2 (equation of motion method) behaves similarly to the NCA2.

IX.3.4
\Gamma
-broadening of the ST

Dressing the main fermion line exclusively with the temperature-independent bubble, namely the first term of Eq. (
168
), amounts to neglecting in the DSO retarded Green’s function the temperature-dependent self-energies

\Sigma_{\sigma\pm}(\epsilon)
, see Eq. (
203
). The only internal process retained here corresponds to the temperature-independent diagrammatic contributions in Ref.
Saptsov and Wegewijs 2014
. The resulting Green’s function is that of a
\Gamma
-broadened version of the ST approximation, where the single-particle energies acquire a broadening
\Gamma
due to the coupling to the leads and reads

\displaystyle G^{r}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon_{k})=\sum_{\eta}\frac{{\rm p}^{\bar{\sigma}}_{\eta}}{\epsilon_{k}-E_{\sigma}(\eta)+{\rm i}\Gamma/2}\;.

(206)

This scheme is the same as the EOM0 reviewed in
de Souza Melo et al. 2019
, which is derived with the EOM method.
With this result for the Green’s function, the general formula Eq. (
130
), gives for the current in the ST approximation

\displaystyle I^{\infty}=

\displaystyle\frac{e}{h}\Gamma_{L}\Gamma_{R}\sum_{\sigma}\int d\epsilon\;[f^{L}_{+}(\epsilon)-f^{R}_{+}(\epsilon)]

(207)

\displaystyle\left[\frac{1-\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle}{(\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma})^{2}+\Gamma^{2}/4}+\frac{\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle}{(\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-U)^{2}+\Gamma^{2}/4}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e}{h}\frac{\Gamma_{L}\Gamma_{R}}{\Gamma}\sum_{\sigma}\Big[(1-\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle)r(\epsilon_{\sigma})+\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle r(\epsilon_{\sigma}+U)\Big]\;,

where
r(x)
is defined in Eq. (
147
).

Figure 22:
Linear conductance within the
\Gamma
-broadened ST, Eq. (
206
)
vs
the gate voltage
\epsilon_{0}-\mu
in the degenerate case
\epsilon_{\sigma}=\epsilon_{0}
. Lowering the temperature, the peaks shrink until
k_{\rm B}T\simeq\Gamma
but they do not move towards the center. Their height grows as
T^{-1}
. The tunnel coupling is
\Gamma=0.01~U
with
\Gamma_{L}=\Gamma_{R}=\Gamma/2
.

In Fig.
22
, we show the linear conductance
G
, obtained by inserting the retarded Green’s function (
\zeta=+1
) of Eq. (
206
) into the general expression (
131
). The linear conductance is plotted, in the degenerate case, where
\epsilon_{\uparrow}=\epsilon_{\downarrow}=\epsilon_{0}
, against the gate voltage. Decreasing the temperature,
G
is suppressed around the particle-hole symmetry point
\epsilon_{0}-\mu=-U/2
, the central region between the two peaks separated by the energy
U
, the separation being independent of the temperature, contrary to the NCA2, cf. Fig.
20
.

IX.4
Infinite-tier schemes I: Neglecting the crossings in the main fermion line (DBA)

From the considerations above, the second-tier approximations well describe the behavior of the SIAM above temperatures of the order
k_{\rm B}T\sim\Gamma
. Also they capture the onset of a zero-bias anomaly at low temperature. However, artifacts occur when the temperature is lowered even further. As shown in the next sections, the problems with the Kondo temperature
T_{\rm K}
and the pinning at the particle-hole symmetry point are mitigated by deepening the hierarchy of internal process including third- and fourth-tier bubble diagrams.

Let us go to the exact formal expression for
\boldsymbol{\phi}
, Eq. (
102
), and assume that the main fermion line does not undergo crossings, which yields a diagonal irreducible propagator in
\sigma
and
\kappa
.
Within this assumption, the equation for
\boldsymbol{\phi}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm DBA}
is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\phi}_{\rm DBA}=[\mathbf{h}^{-1}-\tilde{\mathbf{B}}]^{-1}\;,

(208)

see Eq. (
109
).
The propagator is dressed by internal processes according to the hierarchy of Eq. (
101
) with
\langle\tilde{\mathbf{S}}_{2}\mathbf{v}\rangle\rightarrow\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{2}\equiv\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
, where the block
[\tilde{\mathbf{B}}]^{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\chi}}=\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}(\kappa)\delta_{\sigma^{\prime}\sigma}\delta_{\kappa^{\prime}\kappa}
is given by the contraction of a free propagator dressed by all possible (irreducible) processes, including crossings. The diagrammatic of this dressing is in fact the same as the exact irreducible propagator
\boldsymbol{\phi}
itself, see Fig.
14
.

As the bare bubbles of the NCA2, the dressed bubbles are diagonal in all indexes except for
\eta
, inducing a
2\times 2
structure to the contracted (matrix) function
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\rm DBA}(\kappa)
.
Along similar lines as in the NCA2, the matrix elements of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{\rm DBA}
read

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\sigma\sigma}_{{\rm DBA},\eta\eta}=

\displaystyle\frac{({\rm h}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\bar{\eta}})^{-1}-\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\bar{\eta}}}{[({\rm h}^{\sigma}_{\eta\eta})^{-1}-\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\eta\eta}][({\rm h}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\bar{\eta}})^{-1}-\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\bar{\eta}}]-\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\eta\bar{\eta}}\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\eta}}

(209)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\sigma\sigma}_{{\rm DBA},\bar{\eta}\eta}=

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\eta}}{[({\rm h}^{\sigma}_{\eta\eta})^{-1}-\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\eta\eta}][({\rm h}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\bar{\eta}})^{-1}-\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\bar{\eta}}]-\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\eta\bar{\eta}}\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\eta}}\;,

where the bare block
{\rm B}
has been replaced by the dressed one
\tilde{\rm B}
.
Similar to Eq. (
187
), the dot Green’s function in the
\infty
-tier scheme reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{(\zeta)}_{\sigma\sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-{\rm i}\zeta\hbar\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{--}+{\rm i}\zeta\hbar\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{+-}\frac{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-{\rm i}\zeta\hbar[\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{--}+\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{-+}]}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-U-{\rm i}\zeta\hbar[\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{++}+\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{+-}]}}

(210)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-U-{\rm i}\zeta\hbar\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{++}+{\rm i}\zeta\hbar\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{-+}\frac{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-U-{\rm i}\zeta\hbar[\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{++}+\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{+-}]}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-{\rm i}\zeta\hbar[\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{--}+\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{-+}]}}\;.

As above, the dependence on
\kappa
is understood.
Thus, neglecting the crossing of the main fermion line, we obtain a general structure for the retarded Green’s function by a simple
2\times 2
(block) matrix inversion.
The matrix elements of the dressed bubbles are schematized by

\displaystyle\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}=

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\resizebox{}{2797019}{

{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{1,1,1}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 102.36 -23.62 M 102.36 -23.62 L 102.36 47.24 L 55.12 47.24 L 55.12 -23.62 Z M 55.12 47.24}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}\end{gathered}\;.

(211)

Here, the bare contribution (no internal processes) to the first bubble diagram is diagonal in
\eta^{\prime}\eta
and is also independent of the value of the sojourn
\eta
, as it does not include interactions. This bare bubble is the same as the one of the NCA2 and, in the WBL, is evaluated to be
-\mathbf{1}\Gamma/2\hbar
, see Eq. (
168
). In the presence of internal processes, the first dressed bubble in Eq. (
211
) depends in principle on the sojourn
\eta^{\prime}
. This occurs when the latter has overlap with a blip of the
\sigma
path. However, in this case the block vanishes because the overlap implies overlap of three fermion lines of the state
\sigma
, resulting in a factor
\nu
that makes the whole diagram vanish upon summing over
\nu
, as exemplified in the diagram
(B)
of Eq. (
60
).
As a result, this block is independent of the value of the last sojourn
\eta^{\prime}
. The same independence of
\eta^{\prime}
holds for the second block, because the sojourn
\eta^{\prime}
, although involved with interactions, lies outside the block itself. On the contrary, the initial sojourn is relevant for both bubbles because it determines the vertex of the fermion line of spin
\bar{\sigma}
according to the definition in Eq. (
92
). Summarizing, on the basis of the diagrammatic rules we conclude that the dressed bubble
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
possess the same property of the bare bubble, namely

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}=

\displaystyle-\frac{\Gamma}{2\hbar}\mathbf{1}\delta_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}+\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{\bar{\eta}\eta}\;,

(212)

where, again, boldface objects indicate diagonal matrices in the indexes
\sigma
and
\boldsymbol{\kappa}
.
Exploiting the symmetry in Eq. (
212
), we can simplify the form of the Green’s function in Eq (
210
). In particular, the retarded (
\zeta=+1
) Green’s function in the WBL reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{r}_{\sigma\sigma}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}+{\rm i}\frac{\Gamma}{2}+\tilde{\Sigma}_{\sigma-}(\epsilon)\frac{U}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-U+{\rm i}\frac{\Gamma}{2}-\tilde{\Sigma}_{\sigma}(\epsilon)}}

(213)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\hat{n}_{\bar{\sigma}}\rangle}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}-U+{\rm i}\frac{\Gamma}{2}-\tilde{\Sigma}_{\sigma+}(\epsilon)\frac{U}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\sigma}+{\rm i}\frac{\Gamma}{2}-\tilde{\Sigma}_{\sigma}(\epsilon)}}\;,

where

\tilde{\Sigma}_{\sigma}(\epsilon)=\sum_{\eta}\tilde{\Sigma}_{\sigma\eta}(\epsilon)\;.

(214)

Equation (
213
) is one of the main results of the present work.
The problem of calculating the retarded Green’s function, and thus the relevant physical properties for proportional coupling, reduces to that of determining the retarded, dressed self-energies
\tilde{\Sigma}_{\sigma\eta}
, here identified with the off-diagonal elements of the dressed bubbles via

\displaystyle\tilde{\Sigma}_{\sigma\eta}(\epsilon):={\rm i}\hbar\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\bar{\eta}\eta}(\kappa)|_{\zeta=+1}\;.

(215)

The evaluation of these self-energies remains complicated due to the inner processes dressing the bubble
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\sigma}
.

The retarded Green’s function in Eq. (
213
) has the same form of the one found in
Lavagna 2015
with a self-consistent truncation of the equations of motion. As such, provided that the self-energies have the correct form, the DBA can in principle reproduce the unitary limit at
T=0
. In the degenerate case, the latter is obtained if, at the particle-hole symmetry point
\epsilon_{0}-\mu=-U/2
, the dressed self-energies acquire the values

\tilde{\Sigma}_{\sigma\pm}(\mu)=\pm\frac{\Gamma}{\pi}\ln\left(\frac{2k_{\rm B}T_{\rm K}}{\sqrt{U\Gamma}}\right)+{\rm i}\frac{{\rm Im}\tilde{\Sigma}_{\sigma}(\mu)}{2}

(216)

in terms of the Kondo temperature, Eq. (
199
), so that
{\rm Im}\mathcal{G}^{r}_{\sigma\sigma}(\mu)=-2/\Gamma
.
We next introduce an approximation scheme, the noncrossing approximation (NCA), where also the internal crossings are neglected. Note that this scheme, obtained by systematically neglecting the crossings in the diagrammatic unravelling of the self-energies,
does not coincide
with the NCA well-known in the Green’s functions literature
Wingreen and Meir 1994

IX.5
Infinite-tier schemes II: Neglecting all crossings (NCA)

In the absence of crossings at all levels, the hierarchy in Eq. (
101
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{h}}_{n-1}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Bigg(\mathbf{h}_{n-1}\langle\tilde{\mathbf{h}}_{n}\mathbf{v}\rangle\Bigg)^{k}\mathbf{h}_{n-1}

(217)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\mathbf{h}_{n-1}^{-1}-\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{n}^{\rm NCA}]^{-1}\;,

with
\mathbf{h}_{n}=\mathbf{1}{\rm h}_{n}
denoting the bare propagator with
n
overlapping fermion lines and
\tilde{\mathbf{h}}_{n}
the corresponding propagator dressed by higher-tier bubbles
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{n}^{\rm NCA}=\langle\tilde{\mathbf{h}}_{n}\mathbf{v}\rangle
. In particular
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{2}^{\rm NCA}=\langle\tilde{\mathbf{h}}_{2}\mathbf{v}\rangle\equiv\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
, where
\tilde{\mathbf{h}}_{2}=[\mathbf{h}_{2}^{-1}-\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{3}^{\rm NCA}]^{-1}
. In the following we drop the indexes and make the identification
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{2}^{\rm NCA}\equiv\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
. The dressed bubble
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
in Eq. (
211
) is then schematically described by the following sum of two contributions

\displaystyle\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}=

\displaystyle\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma(\sigma)}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}+\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma(\bar{\sigma})}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}

(218)

\displaystyle=

Here, the white rectangles indicate the dressing of the propagators
\mathbf{h}_{2}
with two overlapping fermion lines by iteration of third-tier bubbles. Specifically, the dressed bubble of type
\sigma(\sigma)
is given by the following contraction of a dressed propagator with
4\times 4
matrix structure in
\boldsymbol{\eta}=(\nu,\eta)

\displaystyle\tilde{\rm B}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}^{\sigma(\sigma)}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu}\langle\sum_{\nu^{\prime}}[\tilde{\mathbf{h}}^{\sigma(\sigma)}_{2}]_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\rm v}_{\nu}\rangle\;,

where
\tilde{\mathbf{h}}_{2}^{\sigma(\sigma)}
is obtained by dressing the bare propagator
\mathbf{1}{\rm h}_{2}^{\sigma(\sigma)}
, Eq. (
165
), with the third-tier bubble
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{3}^{\sigma(\sigma)}
, namely

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{h}}_{2}^{\sigma(\sigma)}=\left[[\mathbf{1}{\rm h}_{2}^{\sigma(\sigma)}]^{-1}-\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{3}^{\sigma(\sigma)}\right]^{-1}\;.

(220)

(221)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu^{\prime}\nu\langle\tilde{\rm h}^{\sigma(\sigma\bar{\sigma})}_{3,\nu^{\prime}\nu}{\rm v}_{-\eta}\rangle+\nu^{\prime}\nu\langle\tilde{\rm h}^{\sigma(\sigma\sigma)}_{3,\eta^{\prime}\eta}{\rm v}_{-\nu}\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\nu^{\prime}\nu\tilde{\rm B}_{3,\nu^{\prime}\nu}^{A}(\eta)+\nu^{\prime}\nu\tilde{\rm B}_{3,\eta^{\prime}\eta}^{B}(\nu)\;.

The prefactor
\nu^{\prime}\nu
stems from overlap of three fermion lines of spin
\sigma
, according to the diagrammatic rules, see Eqs. (
53
)-(
55
) and also Eq. (
309
). Note that these third-tier bubbles can in principle change the occupation state of both spin degrees of freedom. As a result, they capture spin-flip process, which are virtual processes by which the state of the dot with single occupation changes spin due to multiple (virtual) transitions as for example in

\uparrow\;\longrightarrow\;(\uparrow\downarrow)\;\longrightarrow\;\downarrow\;.

We anticipate that, since in the SIAM we deal with two degrees of freedom, the
4\times 4
structure of
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}_{3}^{\sigma(\sigma)}
is the largest in the hierarchical analysis.

The other second-tier bubble in Eq. (
218
) is
\tilde{\rm B}^{\sigma(\bar{\sigma})}
. It is calculated as the contraction of a dressed propagator which bears no structure in the sojourn indexes

\displaystyle\tilde{\rm B}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta}^{\sigma(\bar{\sigma})}=

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{\rm h}_{2}^{\sigma(\bar{\sigma})}{\rm v}_{-\eta}\rangle\;,

(222)

where
\tilde{\rm h}_{2}^{\sigma(\bar{\sigma})}

is obtained by dressing the bare propagator
{\rm h}_{2}^{\sigma(\bar{\sigma})}
in Eq. (
166
) with the third-tier bubble
\tilde{\rm B}_{3}^{\sigma(\bar{\sigma})}
according to

\displaystyle\tilde{\rm h}_{2}^{\sigma(\bar{\sigma})}=\left[[{\rm h}_{2}^{\sigma(\bar{\sigma})}]^{-1}-\tilde{\rm B}_{3}^{\sigma(\bar{\sigma})}\right]^{-1}\;.

(223)

The third-tier bubble entering this equation is

\displaystyle\tilde{\rm B}_{3}^{\sigma(\bar{\sigma})}=
